The VLM processes multi-view images to capture rich, detailed visual information and leverages its common-sense reasoning capabilities to guide the real-time planner in generating robust and safe trajectories. Furthermore, we develop the Context-Adaptive Inference Gate (CAI-Gate) mechanism that enables the VLM to mimic human driving behavior by dynamically adjusting its inference frequency based on scene complexity, thereby achieving an optimal balance between planning performance and computational efficiency. We evaluate our approach on the large-scale, challenging nuPlan benchmark, with comprehensive experimental results demonstrating superior planning performance in scenarios with intricate road conditions and dynamic elements. Code will be available.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary, a critical evaluation of the paper's novelty and significance, and a final score with justification.

**Summary:**

The paper "VLMPlanner: Integrating Visual Language Models with Motion Planning" introduces a novel hybrid framework for autonomous driving motion planning. VLMPlanner combines a learning-based real-time planner with a Vision-Language Model (VLM) that processes raw multi-view images to capture detailed visual information. The VLM guides the real-time planner using its common-sense reasoning capabilities, enabling robust and safe trajectory generation in complex driving environments. A key component is the Context-Adaptive Inference Gate (CAI-Gate), which dynamically adjusts the VLM's inference frequency based on scene complexity, balancing planning performance and computational efficiency. The approach is evaluated on the nuPlan benchmark, demonstrating superior planning performance, particularly in challenging scenarios with intricate road conditions and dynamic elements. The paper also contributes two new datasets, DriveVQA and ReasoningVQA, designed to improve the domain adaptability of VLMs for autonomous driving.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Addressing a Real Limitation:** The paper directly tackles a key weakness of existing LLM-based planning approaches, which often rely on abstracted perception or map-based inputs and, thus, miss critical visual context.
    *   **Hybrid Architecture:** The hybrid architecture, integrating a VLM with a real-time planner, seems a practical and effective approach, leveraging the strengths of both components. This avoids the reliance on LLMs for direct trajectory generation, which is computationally expensive and can suffer from numerical inaccuracies.
    *   **CAI-Gate for Efficiency:** The Context-Adaptive Inference Gate (CAI-Gate) is a significant contribution. It addresses the computational burden of continuously running a VLM, making the approach more viable for real-time applications. This is critical.
    *   **New Datasets:** The creation of DriveVQA and ReasoningVQA is a valuable contribution, specifically tailored for improving VLM understanding of autonomous driving scenarios. This is a welcome step.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The paper demonstrates its approach's effectiveness through rigorous evaluation on the challenging nuPlan benchmark, outperforming state-of-the-art methods in both open-loop and closed-loop settings. The ablation studies provide further insights into the contribution of each component. The qualitative results also bolster the claims.
    *   **Clear and Well-Written:** The paper is generally well-written and clearly explains the proposed approach and the experimental setup.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on a Pre-trained VLM:** The paper relies on a pre-trained VLM (LLaVA-v1.5-7b) and fine-tunes it. While fine-tuning is practical, the overall performance may be limited by the base VLM's capabilities. A more sophisticated VLM or an architecture specifically designed for driving might yield further improvements. The ablation study does test the benefit of the addition pretraining and seems to improve performance.
    *   **NuPlan Specific:**  The evaluation is entirely based on the nuPlan dataset. While nuPlan is a challenging benchmark, demonstrating the approach's generalization capabilities on other datasets or real-world driving scenarios would be valuable. It's difficult to say if the benefits would translate to different sensor configurations or environments.
    *   **Complexity of the CAI-Gate:** While the CAI-Gate is a positive contribution, the paper could benefit from a more detailed explanation of the learning based and rule based methods and hyperparameters.
    *   **Scalability Concerns:** While the CAI-Gate improves efficiency, deploying VLMs in real-time autonomous driving systems remains computationally demanding. The paper could benefit from more discussion of the practical considerations for deployment, such as hardware requirements and power consumption. Also, the ablation study of the gate relies on a rather limited set of tests.
    *   **Dependence on Perception System.** While a strength is that it takes in raw images, it inherently is limited by the capabilities of CLIP. Future work might consider alternative modalities.

*   **Novelty:** The novelty lies in the combination of several aspects: the VLM-integrated hybrid architecture for motion planning using raw image inputs, the CAI-Gate mechanism for dynamic inference adjustment, and the introduction of the DriveVQA and ReasoningVQA datasets. The approach to capturing fine-grained visual details directly from images and integrating them into a real-time planner differentiates it from existing LLM-based methods.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a significant challenge in autonomous driving motion planning by improving scene understanding in complex and long-tail scenarios. The hybrid architecture and the CAI-Gate offer a practical approach to leveraging the power of VLMs while maintaining real-time performance. The new datasets contribute to the development of more domain-specific VLMs for autonomous driving. If the VLMPlanner approach can translate to real-world settings, it could lead to safer and more robust autonomous driving systems.

**Score and Justification:**

I assign a score of **7.5** to this paper.

**Justification:**

The paper presents a well-engineered and empirically validated approach to integrating VLMs into autonomous driving motion planning. The hybrid architecture, CAI-Gate, and new datasets are significant contributions that address real-world challenges and advance the state-of-the-art. The clear presentation and rigorous evaluation further strengthen the paper.

However, the reliance on a pre-trained VLM, the NuPlan-specific evaluation, and the scalability concerns limit the paper's impact. The CAI-gate is relatively shallow and lacks a rigorous validation in the ablation study, and the choice of relying on CLIP is questionable. While the results are promising, further research is needed to demonstrate the approach's generalization and scalability for practical deployment. The limitations do not negate the contributions, but they warrant a more conservative evaluation.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 0/10

### RMTBench: Benchmarking LLMs Through Multi-Turn User-Centric Role-Playing
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.20352v1)
- **Authors**: Hao Xiang, Tianyi Tang, Yang Su, Bowen Yu, An Yang, Fei Huang, Yichang Zhang, Yaojie Lu, Hongyu Lin, Xianpei Han, Jingren Zhou, Junyang Lin, Le Sun
- **Abstract**: Recent advancements in Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown outstanding potential for role-playing applications. Evaluating these capabilities is becoming crucial yet remains challenging. Existing benchmarks mostly adopt a \textbf{character-centric} approach, simplify user-character interactions to isolated Q&A tasks, and fail to reflect real-world applications. To address this limitation, we introduce RMTBench, a comprehensive \textbf{user-centric} bilingual role-playing benchmark featuring 80 diverse characters and over 8,000 dialogue rounds. RMTBench includes custom characters with detailed backgrounds and abstract characters defined by simple traits, enabling evaluation across various user scenarios. Our benchmark constructs dialogues based on explicit user motivations rather than character descriptions, ensuring alignment with practical user applications. Furthermore, we construct an authentic multi-turn dialogue simulation mechanism. With carefully selected evaluation dimensions and LLM-based scoring, this mechanism captures the complex intention of conversations between the user and the character. By shifting focus from character background to user intention fulfillment, RMTBench bridges the gap between academic evaluation and practical deployment requirements, offering a more effective framework for assessing role-playing capabilities in LLMs. All code and datasets will be released soon.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "RMTBench: Benchmarking LLMs Through Multi-Turn User-Centric Role-Playing":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces RMTBench, a novel bilingual (English and Chinese) benchmark for evaluating the role-playing capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs).  Unlike existing benchmarks that primarily focus on character consistency through isolated question-answer pairs, RMTBench adopts a user-centric approach. It designs dialogues around diverse user intentions and needs, simulating more realistic, multi-turn conversations.  The benchmark includes 80 diverse characters (celebrities, fictional, and custom-designed), 8000+ dialogue rounds, and five distinct user-intention-based scenarios: Character Understanding, Character Maintenance, Implicit User Intentions Response, User Preference Awareness and Reasoning, and Sensitive User Behavior Handling.  The authors evaluate several open-source and closed-source LLMs using RMTBench and show that closed-source models perform better overall. They also highlight the impact of language (English vs. Chinese) on model performance and identify areas for improvement in long-context modeling and character maintenance, particularly for open-source LLMs. The authors further showed through ablation experiments that multi-turn settings are crucial for realistic evaluations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper's primary novelty lies in shifting the focus from a character-centric to a user-centric evaluation of LLM role-playing.  This is a significant departure from the traditional approach, which typically measures how well a model can maintain character consistency based on predefined character traits. By centering the evaluation on user intentions and needs, RMTBench offers a more realistic and practically relevant assessment of LLM role-playing capabilities.
    The paper also introduces a more comprehensive categorization of character types (celebrities, fictional, and custom) and a wider range of user intention scenarios than many existing benchmarks. Also novel is its use of only user queries and requiring the LLM to build context within multi-turn dialogue, rather than providing static responses.

*   **Significance:**  RMTBench addresses a critical gap in the evaluation of LLMs for role-playing applications.  Current benchmarks often fall short of capturing the complexity and dynamics of real-world user interactions.  By simulating diverse user intentions and needs within multi-turn conversations, RMTBench provides a more robust and ecologically valid framework for assessing LLM performance. The benchmark's findings highlight the importance of long-context modeling, character maintenance, and sensitivity to user preferences – all crucial factors for developing effective role-playing agents.
    The paper's findings on the impact of language on model performance also have important implications for developing and deploying LLMs in multilingual contexts. RMTBench can become a standard in the evaluation process for dialogue agents and personalized AI.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **User-centric approach:**  A significant improvement over character-centric evaluations.
    *   **Comprehensive and diverse dataset:** The large number of characters, dialogue rounds, and user intention scenarios allows for a thorough evaluation of LLM role-playing capabilities.
    *   **Multi-turn context:**  Simulates real-world interactions better than single-turn or limited-history benchmarks.
    *   **Bilingual support:** The inclusion of both English and Chinese enhances the benchmark's accessibility and relevance for a wider range of users.
    *   **Ablation studies:** Provide insights into the importance of different components of the evaluation.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Automated dialogue generation:** reliance on automated query generation, despite a thorough inspection, may limit the depth of the benchmark. The automated dialogues, while more realistic than simple Q&A, might still miss the subtle nuances of human conversation.
    *   **Evaluation biases** The automated evaluation introduces LLM evaluation biases.
    *   **Complexity**: While RMTBench is a step up, there are still several scenarios that might be difficult to evaluate, e.g., nuanced scenarios that incorporate elements that are difficult to classify.
    *   **Evaluation Metric Coverage**: Limited metrics, may fail to assess role-playing capabilities holistically.

*   **Potential Influence:**  RMTBench has the potential to become a valuable resource for researchers and developers working on role-playing LLMs. The benchmark can be used to:

    *   Evaluate and compare the performance of different LLMs.
    *   Identify areas for improvement in LLM role-playing capabilities.
    *   Develop more effective role-playing agents for various applications, such as entertainment, education, and emotional support.
    *   Advance the understanding of user-AI interaction dynamics.
* **Score:** 8

**Justification:**

RMTBench represents a significant advance over existing role-playing benchmarks due to its user-centric approach, comprehensive dataset, and multi-turn context simulation. The paper is well-written, clearly explains the methodology, and provides valuable insights into the performance of different LLMs. The inclusion of multiple intention based benchmarks, and ablation experiments are significant. The use of automated techniques and limited performance evaluation do limit the scope of the study. Overall, RMTBench offers a valuable framework for evaluating LLMs in a more realistic and practically relevant way, making it a substantial contribution to the field. The limitations are well-defined, which suggest that significant improvements and continued work in this direction will be significant.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Beyond Binary Moderation: Identifying Fine-Grained Sexist and Misogynistic Behavior on GitHub with Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.20358v1)
- **Authors**: Tanni Dev, Sayma Sultana, Amiangshu Bosu
- **Abstract**: Background: Sexist and misogynistic behavior significantly hinders inclusion in technical communities like GitHub, causing developers, especially minorities, to leave due to subtle biases and microaggressions. Current moderation tools primarily rely on keyword filtering or binary classifiers, limiting their ability to detect nuanced harm effectively. Aims: This study introduces a fine-grained, multi-class classification framework that leverages instruction-tuned Large Language Models (LLMs) to identify twelve distinct categories of sexist and misogynistic comments on GitHub. Method: We utilized an instruction-tuned LLM-based framework with systematic prompt refinement across 20 iterations, evaluated on 1,440 labeled GitHub comments across twelve sexism/misogyny categories. Model performances were rigorously compared using precision, recall, F1-score, and the Matthews Correlation Coefficient (MCC). Results: Our optimized approach (GPT-4o with Prompt 19) achieved an MCC of 0.501, significantly outperforming baseline approaches. While this model had low false positives, it struggled to interpret nuanced, context-dependent sexism and misogyny reliably. Conclusion: Well-designed prompts with clear definitions and structured outputs significantly improve the accuracy and interpretability of sexism detection, enabling precise and practical moderation on developer platforms like GitHub.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the issue of sexism and misogyny on GitHub, arguing that existing moderation tools are inadequate due to their reliance on keyword filtering and binary classification. The authors propose a fine-grained, multi-class classification framework using instruction-tuned Large Language Models (LLMs) to identify twelve distinct categories of sexist and misogynistic comments. They iteratively refined prompts for the LLMs, evaluated their performance on a manually annotated dataset of GitHub comments, and analyzed misclassifications to improve accuracy. The optimized approach achieved a Matthews Correlation Coefficient (MCC) of 0.501, outperforming baseline approaches. The paper highlights the importance of well-designed prompts with clear definitions and structured outputs for effective sexism detection and provides lessons learned for developing domain-specific multiclass text classifiers.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a novel application of LLMs to a critical problem in software engineering: detecting and categorizing sexism and misogyny on GitHub. While the use of LLMs for content moderation is not entirely new, the paper's focus on fine-grained, multi-class classification in the context of GitHub communication, combined with a systematic prompt engineering approach, represents a valuable contribution. The explicit focus on moving beyond binary classification and providing explanations for decisions is also a strength. However, it's essential to acknowledge that the study builds upon existing work, particularly the dataset by Sultana et al. The core innovation is in the prompt engineering and evaluation.
*   **Significance:** The problem being addressed is highly significant.  Sexist and misogynistic behaviors are known to negatively impact inclusion and retention in technical communities.  By providing a more nuanced and potentially explainable method for detecting such behaviors, the paper has the potential to improve moderation efforts on platforms like GitHub. The identification of different forms of sexism and misogyny and their distinct implications is crucial for developing tailored intervention strategies. However, the modest MCC score (0.501) achieved by the best-performing model suggests that there's still significant room for improvement in accurately detecting and classifying these behaviors in a real-world setting. The real-world impact depends heavily on the actual deployability and adoption, which are not demonstrated in this paper.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Well-defined Problem:** The paper clearly articulates the problem and its impact on the software engineering community.
    *   **Rigorous Methodology:** The study employs a systematic approach to prompt engineering, with iterative refinement and thorough evaluation using appropriate metrics.
    *   **Practical Insights:** The paper provides valuable lessons learned for developing domain-specific multiclass text classifiers, which can inform future research in this area.
    *   **Publicly Released Resources:**  The public release of prompts, evaluation results, and the dataset is commendable and promotes reproducibility and further research.
    *   The use of MCC metric is appropriate for imbalanced data.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Performance:** The MCC score of 0.501, while an improvement over baselines, indicates that the model's performance is still not very high, particularly for a task as nuanced as detecting sexism and misogyny. There's a need for substantial performance improvements to ensure practical usability.
    *   **Dataset Dependency:** The study relies on a single, existing dataset. Evaluating the model on other GitHub datasets or datasets from similar platforms would strengthen the findings and assess its generalizability.
    *   **Lack of Real-World Evaluation:** The paper lacks an evaluation of the framework in a real-world setting. It's unclear how well the model would perform in practice, where it would need to handle a wider range of language styles, contexts, and user behaviors. A user study or deployment in a GitHub project would increase confidence.
    *   The reliance on the original study's manual labels which might be subjective. While efforts were made, it could still be a bias/reliability concern.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of more effective content moderation tools for software engineering platforms and other online communities.  It demonstrates the value of LLMs and systematic prompt engineering for addressing complex social problems. It serves as a solid benchmark and resource for future development in this important space.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution to addressing a crucial problem in the software engineering community. The novelty comes from the prompt engineering techniques and demonstration in Github environment, building upon existing work and focusing on nuanced, multi-class classification. The paper also delivers practical insights and contributes with publicly released prompts and results. The main shortcoming is the relatively modest performance (MCC = 0.501), which raises questions about the immediate practical applicability. Furthermore, a real-world evaluation is missing. It's also highly dependent on the original manual labeled dataset. Given the above, the assigned score recognizes the significance of the problem, the valuable insights, and the solid approach while acknowledging the performance limitations and scope for future improvement.

Score: 6.5

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 6/10

### Generative Pre-training for Subjective Tasks: A Diffusion Transformer-Based Framework for Facial Beauty Prediction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.20363v1)
- **Authors**: Djamel Eddine Boukhari, Ali chemsa
- **Abstract**: Facial Beauty Prediction (FBP) is a challenging computer vision task due to its subjective nature and the subtle, holistic features that influence human perception. Prevailing methods, often based on deep convolutional networks or standard Vision Transformers pre-trained on generic object classification (e.g., ImageNet), struggle to learn feature representations that are truly aligned with high-level aesthetic assessment. In this paper, we propose a novel two-stage framework that leverages the power of generative models to create a superior, domain-specific feature extractor. In the first stage, we pre-train a Diffusion Transformer on a large-scale, unlabeled facial dataset (FFHQ) through a self-supervised denoising task. This process forces the model to learn the fundamental data distribution of human faces, capturing nuanced details and structural priors essential for aesthetic evaluation. In the second stage, the pre-trained and frozen encoder of our Diffusion Transformer is used as a backbone feature extractor, with only a lightweight regression head being fine-tuned on the target FBP dataset (FBP5500). Our method, termed Diff-FBP, sets a new state-of-the-art on the FBP5500 benchmark, achieving a Pearson Correlation Coefficient (PCC) of 0.932, significantly outperforming prior art based on general-purpose pre-training. Extensive ablation studies validate that our generative pre-training strategy is the key contributor to this performance leap, creating feature representations that are more semantically potent for subjective visual tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel two-stage framework, Diff-FBP, for facial beauty prediction (FBP). The key innovation is replacing the standard ImageNet pre-training with a generative pre-training phase using a Diffusion Transformer on a large, unlabeled dataset of faces (FFHQ). This pre-training teaches the model the underlying data distribution of human faces through a denoising task. The encoder from the pre-trained Diffusion Transformer is then used as a frozen feature extractor, and a lightweight regression head is fine-tuned on the FBP5500 dataset. The authors demonstrate that this generative pre-training approach significantly outperforms existing methods that rely on ImageNet pre-training, achieving state-of-the-art results on the FBP5500 benchmark.  They perform ablation studies to show the importance of the generative pre-training stage.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of facial beauty prediction. Its core strength lies in addressing the domain gap between ImageNet pre-training and the subjective nature of FBP. The idea of using a generative model (Diffusion Transformer) pre-trained on facial data is a logical and effective way to learn features better suited for aesthetic assessment.

**Novelty:**

*   **Generative Pre-training for FBP:** The primary novelty is the application of a Diffusion Transformer for self-supervised generative pre-training specifically for FBP. While generative models have been used for representation learning, their use as frozen feature extractors, pre-trained specifically for facial aesthetic assessment, is novel in this context.
*   **State-of-the-Art Results:**  The paper achieves state-of-the-art performance on a standard benchmark (FBP5500), which demonstrates the practical effectiveness of the proposed approach. The authors show significant performance gains which solidifies the originality and effectiveness of the framework.

**Significance:**

*   **Addressing the Domain Gap:** The paper tackles a critical limitation of existing FBP methods that rely on object classification pre-training. By learning features specifically related to facial structure and aesthetics, the model achieves superior performance.
*   **Potential for Subjective Tasks:** The proposed framework has broader implications for other subjective visual assessment tasks where generic pre-training is insufficient.
*   **Clear Ablation Studies:** The ablation studies provide convincing evidence that the generative pre-training is the key contributor to the improved performance.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Computational Cost:** The authors acknowledge the high computational cost of pre-training Diffusion Transformers. This could limit the accessibility and scalability of the approach.
*   **Dataset Bias:** The reliance on the FFHQ dataset raises ethical concerns about dataset bias and the potential for perpetuating societal beauty standards.
*   **Limited Scope of Evaluation:** While the model performs well on the FBP5500 dataset, further evaluation on other datasets with different demographics or beauty standards would strengthen the claims.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper demonstrates a sound methodology with strong results. While Diffusion Transformers and generative pre-training are established techniques, their application to the specific problem of FBP, along with the significant performance improvements achieved, warrants a high score. It addresses a clear problem, provides a novel solution, and backs it up with solid experimental evidence. The acknowledged limitations, especially concerning dataset bias, should be kept in mind. The overall contribution and potential to advance future research justify a score of **8**.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### Clustering by Attention: Leveraging Prior Fitted Transformers for Data Partitioning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.20369v1)
- **Authors**: Ahmed Shokry, Ayman Khalafallah
- **Abstract**: Clustering is a core task in machine learning with wide-ranging applications in data mining and pattern recognition. However, its unsupervised nature makes it inherently challenging. Many existing clustering algorithms suffer from critical limitations: they often require careful parameter tuning, exhibit high computational complexity, lack interpretability, or yield suboptimal accuracy, especially when applied to large-scale datasets. In this paper, we introduce a novel clustering approach based on meta-learning. Our approach eliminates the need for parameter optimization while achieving accuracy that outperforms state-of-the-art clustering techniques. The proposed technique leverages a few pre-clustered samples to guide the clustering process for the entire dataset in a single forward pass. Specifically, we employ a pre-trained Prior-Data Fitted Transformer Network (PFN) to perform clustering. The algorithm computes attention between the pre-clustered samples and the unclustered samples, allowing it to infer cluster assignments for the entire dataset based on the learned relation. We theoretically and empirically demonstrate that, given just a few pre-clustered examples, the model can generalize to accurately cluster the rest of the dataset. Experiments on challenging benchmark datasets show that our approach can successfully cluster well-separated data without any pre-clustered samples, and significantly improves performance when a few clustered samples are provided. We show that our approach is superior to the state-of-the-art techniques. These results highlight the effectiveness and scalability of our approach, positioning it as a promising alternative to existing clustering techniques.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've reviewed the provided paper and will provide a summary and critical evaluation.

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel clustering approach that leverages Prior Fitted Networks (PFNs), specifically Transformers, for data partitioning. Unlike traditional clustering algorithms that often require parameter tuning, iterative refinement, or distributional assumptions, the proposed method performs clustering in a single forward pass using a pre-trained PFN. The key idea is to provide a small set of pre-clustered samples to the PFN.  The Transformer network then calculates attention between these pre-clustered samples and the remaining unclustered data points, propagating cluster information and predicting cluster assignments for the entire dataset. The authors claim that this meta-learning-based approach eliminates the need for parameter optimization and achieves accuracy that outperforms state-of-the-art clustering techniques. They provide theoretical justification and empirical evidence using benchmark datasets, demonstrating the method's effectiveness and scalability. The paper suggests the method is suitable for data augmentation, semi-supervised learning, and dimensionality reduction.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its application of PFNs, typically used for supervised tasks like classification and forecasting, to the unsupervised problem of clustering. Adapting the meta-learning and amortized inference capabilities of PFNs to guide clustering is a fresh perspective. The idea of using a few pre-clustered samples as "anchors" to guide the clustering of the entire dataset through attention mechanisms is also a unique contribution. The theoretical analysis that describes how the model learns to cluster as the number of known samples increases also shows novelty.

**Significance:** The potential significance of this work stems from several factors:

*   **Efficiency:** The single forward pass inference of PFNs makes the method potentially more efficient than iterative clustering algorithms, especially for large datasets, though the complexity is still O(n^2).
*   **Reduced Parameter Tuning:** The meta-learning approach aims to alleviate the need for extensive parameter tuning often associated with traditional clustering methods.
*   **Minimal Supervision:** The ability to leverage a small number of pre-clustered samples could bridge the gap between unsupervised and semi-supervised learning.
*   **Scalability:** Although not linear, the use of PFNs allows them to apply approximations.

**However, there are some critical aspects that temper my enthusiasm:**

*   **Theoretical Guarantees and Assumptions:** While the paper mentions theoretical justification, it's not clear if any guarantees can be provided regarding the quality of the clustering solution, especially in the absence of pre-clustered samples or with poorly chosen pre-clustered samples.
*   **PFN Training:** The effectiveness of the approach depends heavily on the pre-training of the PFN. The paper doesn't delve deeply into how the PFN is pre-trained and what kind of synthetic data is generated for this. The quality of pre-training directly affects the final clustering performance.
*   **Computational Complexity of Attention:** The reliance on Transformer-based attention mechanisms introduces a quadratic complexity (O(n^2)) which makes this method impractical for very large datasets unless optimizations like FlashAttention or LongFormer are implemented. The paper mentions this limitation, but doesn't offer an effective solution.
*   **Comparison to Existing Techniques:** Even if better than classic methods, the paper does not compare against more recent neural network based clustering techniques.
*   **Evaluation Datasets and Metrics:** The experimental results demonstrate good performance. However, it should be benchmarked on more diverse and complex datasets.
*   **CPU vs GPU running time** The CPU vs GPU are not compared effectively.

**Strengths:**

*   Novel application of PFNs to unsupervised clustering.
*   Use of attention mechanism for cluster propagation.
*   Potential for efficiency and reduced parameter tuning.

**Weaknesses:**

*   Theoretical guarantees are not thoroughly investigated.
*   PFN pre-training details are not clear.
*   Computational complexity remains a concern for very large datasets.
*   Comparison to more current clustering techniques.

**Overall:**

This paper introduces an interesting and potentially promising approach to data clustering. The use of pre-trained PFNs to guide the clustering process is innovative and could lead to more efficient and robust clustering algorithms. However, several aspects need further investigation, including the theoretical underpinnings, the impact of PFN pre-training, and the practical scalability to very large datasets.

**Score: 6**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a novel idea (3 points), but the practical significance is tempered by several unaddressed issues, primarily regarding the complexity of Transformer architectures, the pre-training aspect, and the lack of comparison with more modern clustering techniques.

Even though the performance results are better, there are other NN based clustering techniques that may beat this approach. It may prove better in some niches of clustering with high dimensionality. The paper is decent in most things, but does not excel in any of them.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### MazeEval: A Benchmark for Testing Sequential Decision-Making in Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.20395v1)
- **Authors**: Hafsteinn Einarsson
- **Abstract**: As Large Language Models (LLMs) increasingly power autonomous agents in robotics and embodied AI, understanding their spatial reasoning capabilities becomes crucial for ensuring reliable real-world deployment. Despite advances in language understanding, current research lacks evaluation of how LLMs perform spatial navigation without visual cues, a fundamental requirement for agents operating with limited sensory information. This paper addresses this gap by introducing MazeEval, a benchmark designed to isolate and evaluate pure spatial reasoning in LLMs through coordinate-based maze navigation tasks. Our methodology employs a function-calling interface where models navigate mazes of varying complexity ($5\times 5$ to $15\times 15$ grids) using only coordinate feedback and distance-to-wall information, excluding visual input to test fundamental spatial cognition. We evaluate eight state-of-the-art LLMs across identical mazes in both English and Icelandic to assess cross-linguistic transfer of spatial abilities. Our findings reveal striking disparities: while OpenAI's O3 achieves perfect navigation for mazes up to size $30\times 30$, other models exhibit catastrophic failure beyond $9\times 9$ mazes, with 100% of failures attributed to excessive looping behavior where models revisit a cell at least 10 times. We document a significant performance degradation in Icelandic, with models solving mazes 3-4 sizes smaller than in English, suggesting spatial reasoning in LLMs emerges from linguistic patterns rather than language-agnostic mechanisms. These results have important implications for global deployment of LLM-powered autonomous systems, showing spatial intelligence remains fundamentally constrained by training data availability and highlighting the need for architectural innovations to achieve reliable navigation across linguistic contexts.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "MazeEval: A Benchmark for Testing Sequential Decision-Making in Language Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MazeEval, a new benchmark to evaluate the spatial reasoning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) through coordinate-based maze navigation tasks. Unlike existing benchmarks, MazeEval minimizes sensory input by providing only coordinate feedback and distance-to-wall information. The authors evaluate eight state-of-the-art LLMs in both English and Icelandic to assess cross-linguistic transfer.  Key findings include significant performance variations across models, a performance degradation with increasing maze complexity (often failing after 7x7 mazes), and a surprising performance drop in Icelandic compared to English, suggesting the influence of linguistic training data. The primary failure mode was excessive looping (repeatedly visiting the same cell). OpenAi’s O3 model demonstrated exceptional performance, handling even 30x30 mazes in both languages.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The benchmark itself (MazeEval) is the most significant novel contribution.  Isolating spatial reasoning with minimal sensory input and using a coordinate-based system is a good way to test core capabilities. The comparison between English and Icelandic adds another dimension, addressing a gap in multilingual evaluation in agent benchmarks.  However, the core concept of maze navigation is not entirely new, as grid-world environments and text-based navigation have been explored previously (though usually with richer contextual cues). The multilingual component isn't completely original either; multilingual evaluation is becoming increasingly common for NLP tasks. The use of function calling is a well-established method. The lack of similar data in previous public datasets is a welcome feature.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses an important and timely problem: the deployment of LLMs in embodied AI and robotics. The finding that spatial reasoning capabilities are not purely emergent but depend on linguistic training data has implications for real-world deployment, particularly in low-resource language settings. Identifying excessive looping as a key failure mode is also insightful, highlighting the limitations of current LLM architectures in maintaining spatial coherence. The benchmarking reveals the fragility of spatial reasoning in most LLMs (except a few like O3). The authors accurately highlight how it demonstrates that the training paradigm and architectural choices can still drastically improve performance. They highlight the limitations of spatial working memory in the tested LLMs.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Well-defined benchmark with clear evaluation metrics.
    *   Rigorous evaluation of multiple state-of-the-art LLMs.
    *   Cross-linguistic analysis provides valuable insights into the role of language in spatial reasoning.
    *   Identification of a specific failure mode (excessive looping).
    *   The paper is well-written and easy to follow.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The coordinate system and lack of visual input, while controlled, may not fully reflect real-world scenarios. Real-world tasks often involve processing visual information alongside coordinate data.
    *   The maze environment, while useful for isolating spatial reasoning, is simplified. Results might not generalize directly to more complex, continuous environments.
    *   The scope is limited to 2D mazes.  While this provides a good starting point, extending to 3D environments would increase the benchmark's relevance.
    *   While the authors mention that their prompt provides position history, the length of the provided history and how it affects performance could have been investigated in greater depth. Is the history window sufficient for the LLMs or does increasing it further improve performance?
    *   The paper acknowledges that the success rate analysis are on runs where the models are successful and this can introduce bias on what is being analyzed.

*   **Impact:** The paper will likely influence future research on LLMs for embodied AI by:

    *   Providing a standard benchmark for evaluating spatial reasoning in LLMs.
    *   Highlighting the need for improved spatial reasoning capabilities in LLMs.
    *   Motivating research on cross-linguistic transfer of spatial skills.
    *   Inspiring new architectures and training methods for LLMs that can better maintain spatial coherence.

**Overall Score and Justification:**

The paper makes a useful contribution to the field by introducing a novel benchmark for evaluating spatial reasoning in LLMs and by highlighting the influence of language on this capability. The paper, however, has some limitations in its environment design. The paper has good experimental design and analysis. Therefore the paper will receive the following score:

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Length Representations in Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.20398v1)
- **Authors**: Sangjun Moon, Dasom Choi, Jingun Kwon, Hidetaka Kamigaito, Manabu Okumura
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have shown remarkable capabilities across various tasks, that are learned from massive amounts of text-based data. Although LLMs can control output sequence length, particularly in instruction-based settings, the internal mechanisms behind this control have been unexplored yet. In this study, we provide empirical evidence on how output sequence length information is encoded within the internal representations in LLMs. In particular, our findings show that multi-head attention mechanisms are critical in determining output sequence length, which can be adjusted in a disentangled manner. By scaling specific hidden units within the model, we can control the output sequence length without losing the informativeness of the generated text, thereby indicating that length information is partially disentangled from semantic information. Moreover, some hidden units become increasingly active as prompts become more length-specific, thus reflecting the model's internal awareness of this attribute. Our findings suggest that LLMs have learned robust and adaptable internal mechanisms for controlling output length without any external control.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates how large language models (LLMs) internally encode and control output sequence length.  It presents empirical evidence that multi-head attention mechanisms, particularly in the lower layers of transformer architectures, play a critical role in determining output length. The authors show that these mechanisms can be adjusted in a disentangled manner, allowing control of output length without significantly compromising the informativeness of the generated text. They demonstrate this by scaling specific hidden units within the model and observing the changes in output length and quality.  Further, they find that certain hidden units become increasingly active when length-specific prompts are used, reflecting an internal awareness of length constraints. The study employs sentence summarization as a testbed and experiments with models from the Llama, Phi-3, and Qwen families.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The core novelty lies in the empirical investigation of *how* LLMs internally represent and control output length, a relatively unexplored area. While prior works have focused on external controls (prompt engineering, length priming), this paper dives into the internal mechanisms. The finding that multi-head attention is key and that length information appears to be partially disentangled is a significant contribution. The demonstration of length control via scaling specific hidden units further strengthens this point.

*However*, the paper builds on existing work on mechanistic interpretability. The methods used (neural network regression, analyzing hidden unit activations) are established techniques. The "disentanglement" claim, while promising, could be considered incremental.  It shows *some* disentanglement, but doesn't fully isolate length from semantic information.

**Significance:**

Understanding how LLMs control output length has practical implications for various real-world applications (summarization, translation, dialogue) where length constraints are important. Furthermore, enhancing the interpretability and controllability of LLMs is a major goal in the field. The work provides valuable insights into the inner workings of these models, paving the way for more precise control and potentially more efficient architectures.

The significance could be higher if the paper went beyond demonstration and proposed a method to explicitly *train* LLMs to disentangle length representations or develop a new architecture leveraging these insights. The findings, while interesting, are currently more observational.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Research Question:** The paper focuses on a specific and relevant question.
*   **Empirical Evidence:** It provides strong empirical evidence to support its claims.
*   **Comprehensive Experiments:** The experiments cover multiple models and prompting strategies.
*   **Human Evaluation:** Incorporating human evaluation adds weight to the findings.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Incremental Disentanglement:** The paper demonstrates *partial* disentanglement but doesn't fully isolate length information.
*   **Limited Generalization:**  The findings primarily relate to sentence summarization. Further investigation into other tasks (especially generation tasks which are less constrained) would be helpful.  The limited exploration of architecture changes based on these insights limits its broader significance.
*   **Methodological Limitations:** The use of neural networks for regression may have inherent limitations in discovering deeper relationships that are not well captured by this method. More sophisticated techniques may reveal a more complex picture.
*   **Lack of Architectural Innovation:** While the observations are valuable, the paper doesn't propose a novel LLM architecture or training method that leverages these insights.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a solid empirical study with valuable insights into the internal mechanisms of LLMs. It provides a foundation for future research on length control and disentanglement. However, the incremental nature of the findings and the limited demonstration of how this knowledge can be used to improve LLMs restrict its overall impact.

Score: 6.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### CIgrate: Automating CI Service Migration with Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.20402v1)
- **Authors**: Md Nazmul Hossain, Taher A. Ghaleb
- **Abstract**: Continuous Integration (CI) configurations often need to be migrated between services (e.g., Travis CI to GitHub Actions) as projects evolve, due to changes in service capabilities, usage limits, or service deprecation. Previous studies reported that migration across CI services is a recurring need in open-source development. However, manual migration can be time-consuming and error-prone. The state-of-the-art approach, CIMig, addresses this challenge by analyzing past migration examples to create service-specific rules and produce equivalent configurations across CI services. However, its relatively low accuracy raises concerns about the overall feasibility of automated CI migration using rule-based techniques alone. Meanwhile, Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated strong capabilities in code generation and transformation tasks, suggesting potential to improve the automation, usability, and generalizability of CI configuration migration. This registered report presents a study in which we aim to assess whether CI migration can be improved using LLMs. To this end, we propose CIgrate, an LLM-based framework for automatically migrating CI configurations. We plan to evaluate the performance of CIgrate compared to CIMig as a baseline, in different setups (a) zero-shot/few-shot prompting of LLMs for configuration migration and (b) fine-tuning an LLM on a dataset of already established CI service migrations. We will also seek developer feedback on the quality and usability of the generated configurations. We formulate research questions focusing on the accuracy of LLM-generated migrations versus ground truth and the output of CIMig. The expected contributions include the first LLM-powered approach for CI service migration, a comparative evaluation of its effectiveness compared to rule-based approaches, and insight into leveraging LLMs to support software configuration evolution.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the registered report:

**Summary:**

The registered report outlines a study proposing CIGRATE, a framework using Large Language Models (LLMs) to automate the migration of Continuous Integration (CI) configurations between services like Travis CI and GitHub Actions.  This aims to improve upon existing rule-based approaches like CIMig.  The study plans to evaluate CIGRATE's performance in zero-shot, few-shot, and fine-tuned settings, comparing its output to CIMig using similarity metrics and developer feedback gathered via pull requests. The study seeks to determine if LLMs can offer a more flexible and accurate solution for CI migration, addressing challenges such as complex job matrices and custom environment variables. The goal is to offer actionable insights for CI practitioners and potentially influence the design of future CI tools.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The novelty lies in the application of LLMs to the CI migration problem. While LLMs have been used in various software engineering tasks, their systematic evaluation in CI configuration migration against a rule-based baseline is a significant contribution. The idea of a LLM-based approach that attempts to generalize better than CIMig's example-driven rules is promising.  However, it's important to acknowledge that using LLMs for code generation and transformation, in general, is not a novel idea in itself; the novelty is specific to the CI migration domain. The careful experimental design (comparing zero-shot, few-shot, and fine-tuned LLMs with CIMig) enhances the overall novelty of the work.

**Significance:**  The significance stems from the practical challenges of manual CI configuration migration, which are time-consuming and error-prone. Automating this process can save developers valuable time and improve software development workflows. Furthermore, given the evolution and sometimes deprecation of CI services, an automated migration solution is a recurring need.  A key contribution of the study is the focus on bidirectional migration (Travis CI to GitHub Actions and vice-versa). By directly evaluating LLM-generated configurations with developer feedback through pull requests and considering their willingness to adopt it, the study attempts to bring a more realistic angle. The choice of models, including both open-source and proprietary, is also commendable.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Research Questions:** The research questions are well-defined and focused on evaluating the effectiveness and usability of LLMs for CI migration.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation Plan:** The study includes a thorough evaluation plan, incorporating both automated metrics (Cosine Similarity, CrystalBLEU, CI linters) and developer feedback, offering a balanced view of performance.
*   **Comparison with Baseline:** Comparing CIGRATE with CIMig, a state-of-the-art rule-based approach, provides a meaningful benchmark for evaluating the LLM-based solution.
*   **Real-World Data:** Using a curated dataset of real CI migrations (from the CIMig paper) ensures that the study is relevant and practical.
*   **Consideration of Developer Perception:**  The inclusion of developer feedback as a crucial evaluation metric is excellent as it addresses a critical factor in technology adoption: user acceptance.
*   **Addressing threats to Validity**: The registered report carefully considers potential threats to validity (construct, internal, external, and conclusion validity) and proposes specific mitigation strategies, which demonstrates a robust research methodology.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope:** The study is limited to migration between Travis CI and GitHub Actions. Generalizability to other CI services might be limited.
*   **Potential for Data Bias:** The curated dataset may contain biases that affect the performance of LLMs. Although this is a general problem, it should be carefully considered.
*   **Evaluation metric bias:** The Cosine Similarity may be useful but not sufficient in terms of capturing the semantic differences.
*   **Dependency on LLM capabilities**: The performance of CIGRATE is closely tied to the capabilities of LLMs, which are rapidly evolving. The results may become outdated quickly if models improve significantly.
*   **Limited number of models used:** Only 4 models have been selected for evaluation. It is not clear how the selected models would address all the possible configurations.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the strengths and weaknesses, I assign a score of **7.5**.  The application of LLMs to CI migration is a novel and potentially significant contribution with clear practical relevance. The comprehensive evaluation plan and the use of a real-world dataset strengthen the study. However, the limited scope (Travis CI to GitHub Actions only), potential dataset bias, and dependency on current LLM capabilities temper the enthusiasm. The study could have been strengthened by including different metrics to capture the semantic relations and address the fact that current metrics may not be sufficient to evaluate correctness. Although the scope is limited, the framework will significantly contribute to the CI/CD space.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 7/10

### A General Framework for Estimating Preferences Using Response Time Data
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.20403v1)
- **Authors**: Federico Echenique, Alireza Fallah, Michael I. Jordan
- **Abstract**: We propose a general methodology for recovering preference parameters from data on choices and response times. Our methods yield estimates with fast ($1/n$ for $n$ data points) convergence rates when specialized to the popular Drift Diffusion Model (DDM), but are broadly applicable to generalizations of the DDM as well as to alternative models of decision making that make use of response time data. The paper develops an empirical application to an experiment on intertemporal choice, showing that the use of response times delivers predictive accuracy and matters for the estimation of economically relevant parameters.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper abstract and introduction:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a general methodology for estimating preference parameters from choice and response-time data, applicable beyond the standard Drift Diffusion Model (DDM). The approach uses a loss function quadratic in a class of functions of the model parameters, weighted by response time and choice. It offers fast convergence rates (1/n) for the DDM, an explicit finite-sample estimator, and generalization bounds. The authors apply this to an intertemporal choice experiment, demonstrating improved predictive accuracy and influencing economically relevant parameters like discount factors. The method, specialized to the DDM, reduces to a Logit model when response times are ignored, thus providing a perspective on how response times can augment economic choice data. The authors also expand upon basic DDM with generalizations to random starting positions and consider race models, specifically a log-normal version of the linear ballistic model. Finally, they propose estimation within a threshold discrete choice model framework, leveraging learning linear classifiers.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The core idea of using response times in conjunction with choices to estimate preference parameters is not entirely new. Existing literature (cited in the paper itself) already considers DDMs and other models involving response times. However, the novelty lies in the proposed *general methodology* and its theoretical justification.  Specifically, the quadratic loss function approach, finite sample estimator with 1/n convergence, and generalization bounds are potentially significant contributions. Applying this framework to a variety of choice models (DDM, Extended DDM, Race Models, and Threshold Models) to uncover choice and response time data, adds to the paper's contribution. The empirical application, showing how response time data influences the estimated discount factor, is another practical contribution. The connection made between the DDM's use to the Logit model also highlights the potential applications of this research.

**Significance:** The paper addresses an important challenge in behavioral economics: incorporating non-choice data into preference estimation. If the proposed methodology is robust and performs as claimed, it could significantly impact how economists analyze choice data and estimate parameters. Improved predictive accuracy, demonstrated through the empirical application, is a crucial metric of success. The explicit finite-sample estimator with fast convergence is valuable for practical applications.  The paper's ability to compare different choice models based on the integration of response time highlights the significance of developing a unified approach to analyzing behavior. While existing studies delve into specific models, this paper aims for broader applicability.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Generality vs. Implementation:** The abstract and introduction emphasize the "general framework." The true test of its value will be how easily it can be applied to a wide variety of economic settings and model specifications. The empirical illustration, while promising, is relatively simple and further testing is required to support the claims.
*   **Computational Burden:** The non-convexity of the optimization problem in the general case could be a limitation. While the authors state it can be solved numerically, there will inevitably be a greater burden to solve compared to cases where it is convex.
*   **Assumptions:** The method relies on Assumption 1, which requires the family of functions *G(x,y)* to include the ratio of expected decision to expected response time. The robustness of the method with deviations of the assumption requires exploration.
*   **Practical Impact:** While the authors claim improvements in predicting economic choices, the actual degree of improvement will determine the impact of this research. A small increase in predictive accuracy may not offset the increased complexity of the method compared to conventional choice-based analysis.

**Overall Score:**

Given the potential novelty of the proposed methodology, the empirical application showing promising results, and the focus on a practically important problem, I'd give the paper a score of **7**. The 1/n convergence result, provided the assumptions hold and the complexity can be appropriately managed computationally, makes a valuable contribution. However, the true significance hinges on the framework's adaptability, robustness, and the magnitude of the performance gains compared to existing methods across a variety of domains, which the introduction has not justified extensively enough.
Score: 7

- **Classification**: econ.TH
- **Score**: 7/10

### Cognitive Chain-of-Thought: Structured Multimodal Reasoning about Social Situations
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.20409v1)
- **Authors**: Eunkyu Park, Wesley Hanwen Deng, Gunhee Kim, Motahhare Eslami, Maarten Sap
- **Abstract**: Chain-of-Thought (CoT) prompting helps models think step by step. But what happens when they must see, understand, and judge-all at once? In visual tasks grounded in social context, where bridging perception with norm-grounded judgments is essential, flat CoT often breaks down. We introduce Cognitive Chain-of-Thought (CoCoT), a prompting strategy that scaffolds VLM reasoning through three cognitively inspired stages: perception, situation, and norm. Our experiments show that, across multiple multimodal benchmarks (including intent disambiguation, commonsense reasoning, and safety), CoCoT consistently outperforms CoT and direct prompting (+8\% on average). Our findings demonstrate that cognitively grounded reasoning stages enhance interpretability and social awareness in VLMs, paving the way for safer and more reliable multimodal systems.
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Cognitive Chain-of-Thought (CoCoT), a novel prompting strategy for Vision-Language Models (VLMs) designed to improve their reasoning in social situations. CoCoT scaffolds VLM reasoning into three cognitively inspired stages: perception, situation, and norm. The authors demonstrate that CoCoT outperforms standard Chain-of-Thought (CoT) and direct prompting across multiple multimodal benchmarks, including intent disambiguation, commonsense reasoning, and safety instruction following. The results suggest that structuring VLM reasoning through these stages enhances interpretability and social awareness, leading to safer and more reliable multimodal systems.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of structuring VLM reasoning based on cognitive principles is a valuable contribution. Existing CoT methods, designed for symbolic reasoning, often fail in perceptually grounded social tasks.  The integration of perception, situation, and norm stages directly addresses this limitation and aligns VLM reasoning more closely with human social cognition. While prior work has explored structured reasoning in non-visual LLMs, CoCoT's specific cognitive grounding and application to multimodal tasks represents a clear advance. However, it should be acknowledged that the 4E cognition is already an established field of thought, so the paper is really applying that framework, rather than introducing a fundamentally new cognitive theory.

*   **Significance:** The improved performance across diverse tasks (intent disambiguation, commonsense, safety) highlights the broad applicability of CoCoT. The emphasis on safety instruction following is particularly significant, as it directly addresses a crucial challenge in deploying VLMs in real-world scenarios. The interpretability afforded by the structured stages is another positive aspect, allowing for better understanding of model reasoning processes. Improving the interpretability and social awareness of VLMs paves the way for safer and more reliable real-world applications.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition: The paper clearly articulates the limitations of standard CoT in social VLM tasks.
    *   Cognitively grounded approach: The proposed CoCoT method is well-motivated by cognitive principles.
    *   Strong empirical results: The paper provides comprehensive experimental results across multiple benchmarks, demonstrating consistent improvements over baselines.
    *   Interpretability: The structured stages in CoCoT allow for better understanding of the model's reasoning process.
    *   Addresses safety concerns: The application of CoCoT to safety instruction following is particularly important for real-world deployment.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The specific cognitive science literature it relies on isn't particularly recent. Given the speed of advances in cognitive science, it would be good to see more discussion of related cognitive architectures and their inspiration for CoCoT.
    *   Limited model scope: The experiments are conducted using proprietary models (GPT-40 and Gemini 1.5-Pro).  Replicating results with more accessible, open-source models would strengthen the findings.
    *   Computational overhead: The paper acknowledges that the structured nature of CoCoT can lead to longer input sequences and increased computational overhead. This could be a limitation in resource-constrained settings.
    *   Potential for bias amplification:  While CoCoT enhances interpretability, it may also unintentionally amplify biases present in individual reasoning stages, which necessitates careful curation and auditing.
    *   The experiments involving Ablation on Cognitive Stages do not show much differentiation across different ablations, indicating that the performance is not significantly affected by perception, situation, or Norm components independently.

*   **Potential Influence:** CoCoT is likely to influence future research on VLM reasoning by promoting the integration of cognitive principles into model design. It provides a valuable framework for structuring VLM reasoning in social contexts, which could lead to more reliable and trustworthy AI systems. The insights from this paper could also inform the development of more fine-grained evaluation metrics for social intelligence in VLMs.

*   **Score Justification:** Considering the novelty, significance, strengths, and weaknesses, I assign the paper a score of 7.  The paper offers a valuable and well-executed approach to improving VLM reasoning in social contexts and demonstrates significant empirical gains. However, the dependence on proprietary models, concerns of bias amplification, and the lack of differentiation across Ablation experiments slightly limit its overall impact. While the reliance of this research on proprietary models could decrease its overall impact, the paper's insightful framework will likely influence future research on human-aligned reasoning for VLMs.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### CodeNER: Code Prompting for Named Entity Recognition
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.20423v1)
- **Authors**: Sungwoo Han, Hyeyeon Kim, Jingun Kwon, Hidetaka Kamigaito, Manabu Okumura
- **Abstract**: Recent studies have explored various approaches for treating candidate named entity spans as both source and target sequences in named entity recognition (NER) by leveraging large language models (LLMs). Although previous approaches have successfully generated candidate named entity spans with suitable labels, they rely solely on input context information when using LLMs, particularly, ChatGPT. However, NER inherently requires capturing detailed labeling requirements with input context information. To address this issue, we propose a novel method that leverages code-based prompting to improve the capabilities of LLMs in understanding and performing NER. By embedding code within prompts, we provide detailed BIO schema instructions for labeling, thereby exploiting the ability of LLMs to comprehend long-range scopes in programming languages. Experimental results demonstrate that the proposed code-based prompting method outperforms conventional text-based prompting on ten benchmarks across English, Arabic, Finnish, Danish, and German datasets, indicating the effectiveness of explicitly structuring NER instructions. We also verify that combining the proposed code-based prompting method with the chain-of-thought prompting further improves performance.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "CodeNER," a novel prompting method designed to improve the performance of Large Language Models (LLMs) in Named Entity Recognition (NER). Unlike traditional text-based prompting, CodeNER utilizes code-based prompts that explicitly incorporate the BIO (Begin, Inside, Outside) schema labeling instructions within a structured code format. The authors argue that this approach leverages LLMs' ability to understand programming language structures, leading to a better capture of entity boundaries. They compare CodeNER against vanilla text-based prompting, including variations with chain-of-thought prompting, across several NER benchmarks in different languages using both closed-source (ChatGPT) and open-source (Llama-3, Phi-3) models. The experimental results indicate that CodeNER generally outperforms text-based prompting methods, especially in tasks requiring long-range scopes and precise boundary detection, and can be further improved by incorporating CoT. The authors also conduct case studies and ablation experiments to analyze the impact of the BIO schema and the role of different programming languages within the prompting structure.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's core novelty lies in using code-based prompting for NER, specifically to embed the BIO schema. While previous work has explored code-based prompting in NLP, its direct application to improve NER performance by structuring BIO labeling instructions is a key distinguishing aspect. Also, it explores the potential for enhancing NER performance with various prompting methods and LLMs, such as incorporating code-based techniques and assessing zero-shot capabilities. The inclusion of open-source LLMs (Llama-3, Phi-3) for evaluation adds to the practical relevance of the work.

*   **Significance:** The significance of CodeNER stems from addressing a fundamental gap between the text-in-text-out nature of LLMs and the text-in-span-out requirement of NER. By providing a structured way to guide the sequential labeling process, the paper demonstrates a potential path toward improving NER accuracy using LLMs in zero-shot and few-shot scenarios. Furthermore, this approach offers a more explicit and interpretable way to define NER requirements compared to purely text-based prompting.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The paper presents comprehensive experimental results across multiple datasets and LLMs, demonstrating the effectiveness of CodeNER over text-based prompting in most cases.
    *   **Thorough Analysis:**  The ablation studies (e.g., CodeNER w/o label) and case studies provide valuable insights into the impact of the BIO schema and the behavior of CodeNER in different scenarios.
    *   **Practical Implications:** The use of open-source LLMs, in addition to ChatGPT, makes the approach more accessible and encourages further research.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limitations of CodeNER:**  The paper acknowledges that CodeNER's effectiveness is tied to the underlying capabilities of LLMs, and it might require fine-tuning for LLMs not trained in programming languages. The analysis reveals that CodeNER can be less effective in datasets with long sentences or spans that primarily consist of function words, where simple context is more helpful.
    *   **Variable performance:** The performance of codeNER underperforms on some benchmark datasets such as 'CoNLL03' and 'DANE'.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by introducing a new prompting paradigm that bridges the gap between the capabilities of LLMs and the requirements of sequence labeling tasks like NER. This approach could inspire further research in code-based prompting for other NLP tasks and encourage the development of LLMs with better code comprehension. The paper also motivates further exploration of ways to incorporate structured constraints into LLM prompts for NER and other related tasks.

*   **Justification of the score:** The paper presents a well-executed idea, strong empirical results, and a thorough analysis, which together contribute to the advancement of NER via LLMs. The clear structure of the paper and accessibility by providing both closed and open source LLMs for experiments, makes this a solid contribution. Considering the well-reasoned justifications and its potential impact, I assign the following score:

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### When Prompts Go Wrong: Evaluating Code Model Robustness to Ambiguous, Contradictory, and Incomplete Task Descriptions
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.20439v1)
- **Authors**: Maya Larbi, Amal Akli, Mike Papadakis, Rihab Bouyousfi, Maxime Cordy, Federica Sarro, Yves Le Traon
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated impressive performance in code generation tasks under idealized conditions, where task descriptions are clear and precise. However, in practice, task descriptions frequently exhibit ambiguity, incompleteness, or internal contradictions. In this paper, we present the first empirical study examining the robustness of state-of-the-art code generation models when faced with such unclear task descriptions. We extend the HumanEval and MBPP benchmarks by systematically introducing realistic task descriptions flaws through guided mutation strategies, producing a dataset that mirrors the messiness of informal developer instructions. We evaluate multiple LLMs of varying sizes and architectures, analyzing their functional correctness and failure modes across task descriptions categories. Our findings reveal that even minor imperfections in task description phrasing can cause significant performance degradation, with contradictory task descriptions resulting in numerous logical errors. Moreover, while larger models tend to be more resilient than smaller variants, they are not immune to the challenges posed by unclear requirements. We further analyze semantic error patterns and identify correlations between description clarity, model behavior, and error types. Our results underscore the critical need for developing LLMs that are not only powerful but also robust to the imperfections inherent in natural user tasks, highlighting important considerations for improving model training strategies, designing more realistic evaluation benchmarks, and ensuring reliable deployment in practical software development environments.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the robustness of large language models (LLMs) in code generation when faced with task descriptions that are ambiguous, contradictory, or incomplete.  The authors create a dataset by systematically introducing these flaws into existing benchmarks (HumanEval and MBPP) using a guided mutation strategy.  They evaluate various LLMs (different sizes and architectures) on this new dataset, analyzing functional correctness and error modes.  The key findings are that even minor imperfections in task descriptions can significantly degrade performance, that contradictory descriptions lead to logical errors, and that while larger models are more resilient, they aren't immune to these issues. The authors also analyze semantic error patterns and find correlations between description clarity, model behavior, and error types.  The paper argues for the critical need to develop LLMs that are robust to the inherent imperfections of natural user tasks, and suggests directions for improving model training, evaluation benchmarks, and deployment in software development.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in its systematic approach to creating a dataset of flawed task descriptions for code generation. While previous work has looked at robustness, it's often focused on surface-level perturbations or new task domains.  This paper directly addresses the issue of ambiguity, contradiction, and incompleteness, which are common in real-world requirements. The guided mutation strategy, while using GPT-4, provides a structured way to generate these flaws, which is a valuable contribution.

*   **Significance:** The findings have significant implications for the practical application of LLMs in software development. The paper demonstrates that current LLMs are fragile with respect to requirement quality, potentially leading to unreliable code generation.  Highlighting this fragility is important for developers who are considering using LLMs for code generation; it shows that careful attention must be paid to requirements engineering and that some level of human oversight is still crucial. The error analysis also helps identify specific areas where LLMs struggle (e.g., ambiguous descriptions leading to semantic errors), pointing to potential areas for improvement.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Systematic Approach:** The guided mutation strategy provides a well-defined method for creating flawed task descriptions.
    *   **Realistic Flaws:**  The paper focuses on ambiguity, contradiction, and incompleteness, which are well-recognized problems in requirements engineering.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper evaluates multiple LLMs, comparing their performance across different flaw types and model sizes.
    *   **Error Analysis:** The analysis of error patterns provides valuable insights into the specific challenges that LLMs face with unclear task descriptions.
    *   **Actionable Recommendations:** The paper provides suggestions for improving model training, evaluation benchmarks, and deployment strategies.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **GPT-4 Dependence:** The mutation strategy relies on GPT-4, which could introduce biases in the generated task descriptions. Though manual verification helps mitigate this, some subtle biases may remain.
    *   **Limited Scope:** The study focuses on Python code generation for relatively small functions.  It's not clear how well the findings generalize to other programming languages or more complex software systems.
    *   **Metric Limitations:**  The use of *Pass@1* might not fully capture the nuances of code quality. A solution might not pass all tests but still be valuable with minor modifications.

*   **Impact:** The paper is likely to influence research on code generation and requirements engineering.  It highlights the importance of robustness in LLMs and provides a dataset and methodology for evaluating this robustness.
