### Beyond Text: Probing K-12 Educators' Perspectives and Ideas for Learning Opportunities Leveraging Multimodal Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.20720v1)
- **Authors**: Tiffany Tseng, Katelyn Lam, Tiffany Lin Fu, Alekhya Maram
- **Abstract**: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) are beginning to empower new user experiences that can flexibly generate content from a range of inputs, including images, text, speech, and video. These capabilities have the potential to enrich learning by enabling users to capture and interact with information using a variety of modalities, but little is known about how educators envision how MLLMs might shape the future of learning experiences, what challenges diverse teachers encounter when interpreting how these models work, and what practical needs should be considered for successful implementation in educational contexts. We investigated educator perspectives through formative workshops with 12 K-12 educators, where participants brainstormed learning opportunities, discussed practical concerns for effective use, and prototyped their own MLLM-powered learning applications using Claude 3.5 and its Artifacts feature for previewing code-based output. We use case studies to illustrate two contrasting end-user approaches (teacher-and student-driven), and share insights about opportunities and concerns expressed by our participants, ending with implications for leveraging MLLMs for future learning experiences.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores K-12 educators' perspectives and ideas for using multimodal large language models (MLLMs) in learning. Through workshops with 12 educators, the study investigates their ideas for learning opportunities, challenges encountered, and views on student use of MLLMs. Educators brainstormed learning applications and prototyped activities using Claude 3.5, focusing on its text and image input capabilities and interactive output features. The findings highlight educators' excitement about multimedia engagement, concerns about reliance on text, and variations in the types of learning applications that they envision. The authors also discuss potential improvements and concerns around MLLM use.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper addresses a relatively unexplored area: empirical investigation of K-12 educators' perspectives *specifically* on MLLMs. While there is work on AI in education and LLMs in general, a focused examination of educators' views *before* widespread MLLM adoption is a timely contribution. The use of hands-on prototyping with Claude 3.5 further enhances the novelty. However, the general themes are somewhat predictable (e.g., educators are excited about multimedia, concerned about student overreliance), which diminishes the novelty.

*   **Significance:** The study offers practical insights for designing MLLMs for educational settings and for developing professional development resources for teachers. Identifying challenges non-experts encounter when working with MLLMs is crucial for ensuring equitable and effective integration of these technologies. The paper highlights a real need to understand how technology can be seamlessly introduced into a learning environment. It provides an early look into this burgeoning domain. However, the sample size is small, limiting generalizability. Also, the rapid evolution of MLLMs means findings could become dated quickly.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Empirical approach: The study moves beyond theoretical discussions by engaging teachers in hands-on prototyping.
    *   Focus on educator perspectives: It directly addresses the gap in understanding how educators envision MLLMs in their classrooms.
    *   Detailed case studies: The case studies provide rich qualitative data illustrating educator interactions and design processes.
    *   Clear research questions: The research questions provide a roadmap for analysis.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Small sample size: 12 participants limit generalizability.
    *   Model-specific limitations: Findings are specific to Claude 3.5 and may not generalize to other MLLMs.
    *   Limited exploration of challenges: While challenges are mentioned, a deeper investigation of practical obstacles would have strengthened the study.
    * Lack of generalizability: Since technology evolves fast, the findings would be relevant in the near-term.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper can inform the design of more user-friendly MLLM interfaces for education, guide the development of targeted teacher training programs, and promote more nuanced discussions about the ethical and pedagogical implications of AI in schools. It may stimulate more studies with larger and more diverse teacher populations.

* **Justification:** The paper makes a valuable contribution by offering an early empirical exploration of an emerging technology in an educational context. However, the small sample size, limitations specific to the chosen model, and relatively predictable themes temper the degree of innovation.

### AIComposer: Any Style and Content Image Composition via Feature Integration
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.20721v1)
- **Authors**: Haowen Li, Zhenfeng Fan, Zhang Wen, Zhengzhou Zhu, Yunjin Li
- **Abstract**: Image composition has advanced significantly with large-scale pre-trained T2I diffusion models. Despite progress in same-domain composition, cross-domain composition remains under-explored. The main challenges are the stochastic nature of diffusion models and the style gap between input images, leading to failures and artifacts. Additionally, heavy reliance on text prompts limits practical applications. This paper presents the first cross-domain image composition method that does not require text prompts, allowing natural stylization and seamless compositions. Our method is efficient and robust, preserving the diffusion prior, as it involves minor steps for backward inversion and forward denoising without training the diffuser. Our method also uses a simple multilayer perceptron network to integrate CLIP features from foreground and background, manipulating diffusion with a local cross-attention strategy. It effectively preserves foreground content while enabling stable stylization without a pre-stylization network. Finally, we create a benchmark dataset with diverse contents and styles for fair evaluation, addressing the lack of testing datasets for cross-domain image composition. Our method outperforms state-of-the-art techniques in both qualitative and quantitative evaluations, significantly improving the LPIPS score by 30.5% and the CSD metric by 18.1%. We believe our method will advance future research and applications. Code and benchmark at https://github.com/sherlhw/AIComposer.
- **Summary**: ### Paper Summary: The paper titled "AIComposer: Any Style and Content Image Composition via Feature Integration" addresses the challenges of cross-domain image composition using pre-trained T2I (Text-to-Image) diffusion models. While existing methods have made strides in same-domain compositions, this work significantly advances the field by presenting a novel method that allows seamless image composition without relying on text prompts, tackling issues such as style discrepancies and the stochastic nature of diffusion models.  The proposed method utilizes a multilayer perceptron for integrating CLIP features from foreground and background images and employs a local cross-attention mechanism to maintain the integrity of the foreground content during the stylization process. This approach is efficient, requiring minimal steps for backward inversion and forward denoising, and does not necessitate training a separate stylization network. To support the evaluation of their method, the authors introduce a benchmark dataset with a variety of content and styles, filling a current gap in the literature. The experimental results indicate that their method outperforms existing state-of-the-art techniques by significant margins in both qualitative and quantitative measures.  ### Critical Evaluation of Novelty and Significance: **Novelty:** The paper brings notable innovation by addressing cross-domain image composition without text prompts. This is a critical advancement, as most existing methods rely heavily on text, which limits their applicability. The integration of features through a local cross-attention strategy contributes to a new approach that effectively resolves some limitations of traditional methods. Furthermore, adding a benchmark dataset enhances the paper's contribution by providing a resource for future research. **Strengths:** 1. **Methodological Innovation:** By removing the dependency on text prompts and introducing a novel feature integration approach, the paper makes a significant contribution to the field. 2. **Benchmark Dataset:** Creating a dataset specifically for cross-domain composition promotes further research and standardization in evaluations. 3. **Performance Metrics:** Clear improvements over existing techniques in qualitative and quantitative metrics solidify the method's efficacy. **Weaknesses:** 1. **Stochastic Nature:** While the authors acknowledge the stochastic nature of diffusion models, there might be instances where their method could yield inconsistent results, which needs further exploration and discussion. 2. **Limited Scope of Application:** The discussion could expand on the types of images and styles best suited for the proposed method, as well as any constraints that may arise in specific use cases. 3. **Complexity of Implementation:** While the method is described as efficient, the practical implementation may require a deeper dive into the computational resources needed compared to existing techniques. **Overall Significance:** The proposed method has the potential to influence the field significantly by providing a pathway for applications where text descriptions are impractical, and the performance improvements may encourage broader adoption of cross-domain image composition methods in future projects. Given the novelty of the approach and its proven effectiveness, the work is timely and relevant in the landscape of computer vision innovations. **Score: 8**  This score reflects a strong contribution to the field, driven by innovation and practical utility, while acknowledging the need for further investigation into specific limitations and implementation considerations.
- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### Investigating Structural Pruning and Recovery Techniques for Compressing Multimodal Large Language Models: An Empirical Study
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.20749v1)
- **Authors**: Yiran Huang, Lukas Thede, Massimiliano Mancini, Wenjia Xu, Zeynep Akata
- **Abstract**: While Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) demonstrate impressive capabilities, their substantial computational and memory requirements pose significant barriers to practical deployment. Current parameter reduction techniques primarily involve training MLLMs from Small Language Models (SLMs), but these methods offer limited flexibility and remain computationally intensive. To address this gap, we propose to directly compress existing MLLMs through structural pruning combined with efficient recovery training. Specifically, we investigate two structural pruning paradigms--layerwise and widthwise pruning--applied to the language model backbone of MLLMs, alongside supervised finetuning and knowledge distillation. Additionally, we assess the feasibility of conducting recovery training with only a small fraction of the available data. Our results show that widthwise pruning generally maintains better performance in low-resource scenarios with limited computational resources or insufficient finetuning data. As for the recovery training, finetuning only the multimodal projector is sufficient at small compression levels (< 20%). Furthermore, a combination of supervised finetuning and hidden-state distillation yields optimal recovery across various pruning levels. Notably, effective recovery can be achieved with as little as 5% of the original training data, while retaining over 95% of the original performance. Through empirical study on two representative MLLMs, i.e., LLaVA-v1.5-7B and Bunny-v1.0-3B, this study offers actionable insights for practitioners aiming to compress MLLMs effectively without extensive computation resources or sufficient data.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates structural pruning and recovery techniques for compressing Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs). It explores layerwise and widthwise pruning applied to the language model backbone of MLLMs, along with supervised fine-tuning and knowledge distillation for recovery. A key finding is that widthwise pruning is more effective in low-resource scenarios, while a combination of supervised fine-tuning and hidden-state distillation provides optimal recovery across various pruning levels.  The paper also demonstrates that effective recovery can be achieved with a small fraction of the original training data. The study is performed on LLaVA-v1.5-7B and Bunny-v1.0-3B and offers actionable insights for practitioners seeking to compress MLLMs efficiently. A decision flow is also proposed to guide selection of the pruning and recovery method.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Practical Relevance:** The paper addresses a critical need in the MLLM space – reducing computational costs and deployment barriers. This is a highly relevant and practically important research area.
    *   **Systematic Evaluation:** The authors conduct a thorough empirical study, systematically evaluating various pruning and recovery strategies across two representative MLLMs. The analysis is comprehensive, considering different compression ratios, recovery methods, and data availability scenarios.
    *   **Actionable Insights:** The paper provides clear and actionable insights for practitioners, summarizing the effectiveness of different techniques in different settings (e.g., low-resource, high compression). The proposed decision flowchart is a valuable tool.
    *   **Data Efficiency Investigation:** The study of data efficiency in recovery training is particularly valuable. Discovering that minimal data can suffice at moderate compression levels has significant implications for reducing the overall computational cost of compression.
    *   **Reproducibility:** The authors provide details about the experimental setup and implementation, likely increasing reproducibility.
    *   **Generalizability**: The analysis and conclusion have been validated on an additional LLM providing generalizability.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Novelty in Techniques:** The core pruning and knowledge distillation techniques are not entirely novel. While applying them to MLLMs is a valuable contribution, the individual components have been explored in the context of LLMs and other deep learning models.
    *   **Model Selection:** While LLaVA and Bunny are representative, the study could benefit from a broader range of MLLMs to assess the generalizability of the findings fully. The third LLM used for generalizability is in appendix and not fully considered.
    *   **Limited Scope of Recovery Techniques:** While supervised fine-tuning and knowledge distillation are standard recovery methods, the paper could explore more advanced techniques or novel combinations thereof.
    *   **Qualitative Analysis:** While the quantitative analysis is thorough, the qualitative analysis could be enhanced to provide a deeper understanding of how different pruning and recovery strategies affect the model's reasoning abilities and visual understanding.
    *   **Lack of Theoretical Justification:** The paper is primarily empirical. A more substantial theoretical grounding or explanation for why certain techniques perform better in specific scenarios would strengthen the contribution.
    *   **Ablation:** There is some ablation done but further analysis on which intermediate layers the distillation has the greatest impact would be helpful.

*   **Significance:**
    *   The work provides a valuable empirical understanding of how to effectively compress MLLMs without significant performance degradation.
    *   The insights contribute to making MLLMs more accessible and deployable in resource-constrained environments.
    *   The systematic evaluation and practical guidelines can accelerate future research in MLLM compression.

**Justification of Score:**

I assign a score of 7.  The paper addresses a relevant and important problem with practical solutions. It provides a systematic empirical analysis that generates useful, actionable insights. While the individual techniques are not groundbreaking, their application and careful evaluation in the MLLM domain is valuable. The limitations in terms of the scope of models and recovery methods, combined with the lack of strong theoretical justification, prevent the paper from achieving a higher score. However, the practical significance and the thoroughness of the empirical study warrant a score above the median.

### Multilingual Self-Taught Faithfulness Evaluators
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.20752v1)
- **Authors**: Carlo Alfano, Aymen Al Marjani, Zeno Jonke, Amin Mantrach, Saab Mansour, Marcello Federico
- **Abstract**: The growing use of large language models (LLMs) has increased the need for automatic evaluation systems, particularly to address the challenge of information hallucination. Although existing faithfulness evaluation approaches have shown promise, they are predominantly English-focused and often require expensive human-labeled training data for fine-tuning specialized models. As LLMs see increased adoption in multilingual contexts, there is a need for accurate faithfulness evaluators that can operate across languages without extensive labeled data. This paper presents Self-Taught Evaluators for Multilingual Faithfulness, a framework that learns exclusively from synthetic multilingual summarization data while leveraging cross-lingual transfer learning. Through experiments comparing language-specific and mixed-language fine-tuning approaches, we demonstrate a consistent relationship between an LLM's general language capabilities and its performance in language-specific evaluation tasks. Our framework shows improvements over existing baselines, including state-of-the-art English evaluators and machine translation-based approaches.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces STEMF (Self-Taught Evaluators for Multilingual Faithfulness), a novel framework for training multilingual faithfulness evaluators. It addresses the need for accurate, language-agnostic evaluation systems for large language models (LLMs), particularly in the context of increasing LLM adoption in multilingual settings. STEMF leverages synthetic multilingual summarization data and cross-lingual transfer learning to train evaluators without requiring extensive human-labeled data. The paper conducts comprehensive experiments, comparing language-specific and mixed-language fine-tuning approaches, and demonstrates the effectiveness of their framework in improving evaluation performance across multiple languages. A multilingual finetuned faithfulness evaluator based on a 9B-parameter LLM is also presented, which outperforms baselines of similar dimension and matches the performance of larger models. Finally, the paper performs comparison to baselines such as out-of-the-box prompting and translation-based approaches.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Addresses a significant gap:** The paper directly tackles the challenge of evaluating LLM faithfulness in multilingual settings, an area where existing methods are often limited or require substantial resources.
*   **Novel approach:** The STEMF framework presents a creative solution by leveraging synthetic data and cross-lingual transfer learning, reducing the reliance on expensive human annotations. The self-taught approach is well-justified and builds upon existing research.
*   **Comprehensive experimentation:** The experiments are well-designed and cover a range of languages, model architectures, and training strategies. This thoroughness provides strong evidence for the effectiveness of the STEMF framework.
*   **Performance gains:** The results demonstrate that STEMF-trained evaluators outperform existing baselines, including state-of-the-art English evaluators and machine translation-based approaches. This is a significant achievement.
*   **Practical insights:** The paper provides valuable insights for researchers and practitioners working on multilingual LLM evaluation, including considerations for optimal training strategies and the relationship between LLM language capabilities and evaluation performance.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Reliance on synthetic data:** While synthetic data addresses the issue of human annotation costs, the quality of the synthetic data is a crucial factor. The paper could benefit from a more detailed analysis of the quality and representativeness of the generated synthetic data.
*   **Limited scope of tasks:** The evaluation is primarily focused on summarization and RAG tasks. It's unclear how well the STEMF framework would generalize to other LLM applications, such as dialogue generation or creative writing.
*   **Lack of fine-grained analysis of errors:** Although the approach uses labels for specific error types during training, the paper presents little analysis of the types of errors STEMF is particularly good (or bad) at detecting. Knowing this would help with understanding the limitations of the approach.
*   **Ablation study on prompt engineering:** There could be more ablation studies on the sensitivity of the results to different prompt designs.
*   **Scalability to low-resource languages:** While the framework addresses multilingualism, the performance on extremely low-resource languages needs more attention. The reliance on cross-lingual transfer learning might not be sufficient for languages with very limited data.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The paper contributes significantly to the field by introducing a practical and effective approach for training multilingual faithfulness evaluators. The use of synthetic data and cross-lingual transfer learning is a novel combination that addresses a key challenge in LLM evaluation. The performance gains demonstrated in the experiments are substantial and highlight the potential of the STEMF framework. The paper's insights into optimal training strategies and the relationship between LLM language capabilities and evaluation performance are valuable contributions that can inform future research and development in this area.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a solid contribution to the field of multilingual LLM evaluation. The approach is novel, well-executed, and demonstrates significant performance gains over existing baselines. The paper also provides practical insights that can inform future research and development in this area. However, the reliance on synthetic data, the limited scope of tasks, and the need for further investigation into the performance on low-resource languages are limitations that prevent the paper from achieving a higher score. Also, while the results are promising, the overall impact of the results is somehow limited by the evaluation dataset selection. A few more "high-profile" tasks would have increased the paper's impact.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### How Chain-of-Thought Works? Tracing Information Flow from Decoding, Projection, and Activation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.20758v1)
- **Authors**: Hao Yang, Qinghua Zhao, Lei Li
- **Abstract**: Chain-of-Thought (CoT) prompting significantly enhances model reasoning, yet its internal mechanisms remain poorly understood. We analyze CoT's operational principles by reversely tracing information flow across decoding, projection, and activation phases. Our quantitative analysis suggests that CoT may serve as a decoding space pruner, leveraging answer templates to guide output generation, with higher template adherence strongly correlating with improved performance. Furthermore, we surprisingly find that CoT modulates neuron engagement in a task-dependent manner: reducing neuron activation in open-domain tasks, yet increasing it in closed-domain scenarios. These findings offer a novel mechanistic interpretability framework and critical insights for enabling targeted CoT interventions to design more efficient and robust prompts. We released our code and data at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/cot-D247.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper abstract and sections:

**Summary:**

The paper aims to understand the inner workings of Chain-of-Thought (CoT) prompting, a technique that improves large language model (LLM) reasoning. The approach involves tracing information flow through decoding, projection, and activation phases of the LLM. The study uses quantitative analysis and mechanistic interpretability methods to investigate how CoT functions. The key findings include: (1) CoT may prune the decoding space using answer templates, (2) CoT leads to concentrated probability distributions minimizing prediction uncertainty, and (3) CoT modulates neuron engagement in a task-dependent manner, decreasing activation in open-domain tasks but increasing it in closed-domain tasks. The authors suggest that this mechanistic understanding can lead to more efficient and robust prompts.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper attempts to provide a mechanistic explanation for CoT, which is a significant and novel direction. While prior work has suggested various hypotheses for CoT's efficacy (e.g., reducing task complexity, imitating templates), this paper aims to directly link LLM internal states to observed outcomes through mechanistic interpretability. The specific findings, such as CoT modulating neuron activation *differently* across open and closed domains, are potentially new and intriguing observations.
*   **Significance:**  Understanding CoT is crucial for improving and controlling LLMs. If the authors' findings are robust, they could lead to more targeted interventions to enhance CoT's performance and efficiency, and guide the design of new prompts.  The task-dependent neuron engagement is especially noteworthy.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Mechanistic Approach:** Applying mechanistic interpretability to CoT is a strong methodological choice, going beyond input-output correlations.
    *   **Comprehensive Analysis:** The study considers multiple models, datasets, and phases of processing (decoding, projection, activation).
    *   **Specific Findings:**  The paper provides concrete hypotheses about CoT's function (decoding space pruning, uncertainty reduction, neuron modulation).

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Generalizability/Causality:** As the authors themselves acknowledge in the "Limitations" section, establishing definitive causal links in LLMs is incredibly difficult. The paper likely relies on correlations, and demonstrating that the observed effects are genuinely *causing* the improved performance, rather than just being correlated with it, is a challenge.
    *   **Interpretability Challenges:** Even with mechanistic approaches, understanding high-dimensional representations and billions of parameters is a tough ask. The neuron engagement findings, while interesting, may be hard to fully explain or exploit.
    *   **Limited Scope:**  The reliance on vanilla CoT and specific models may limit the generalizability to other variations of CoT or different architectures. The choice of "answer is" as the key point of measurement also seems very specific and may miss out on critical variations in question and answer structure.

*   **Potential Influence:** The work can influence the community by:
    *   Encouraging more mechanistic interpretability research on prompting techniques.
    *   Providing concrete hypotheses to test and refine.
    *   Motivating more targeted interventions in LLMs to control reasoning.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper offers a potentially valuable contribution by applying mechanistic interpretability to understand CoT prompting. It uncovers novel and testable hypotheses related to decoding space pruning, uncertainty reduction, and task-dependent neuron engagement. However, the inherent limitations of LLM interpretability, the correlational nature of the findings, and the specific focus on vanilla CoT temper the overall impact. While the work has good potential for future research, it is not a completely groundbreaking result, and is limited by its correlational nature.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 6/10

### Watermarking Large Language Model-based Time Series Forecasting
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.20762v1)
- **Authors**: Wei Yuan, Chaoqun Yang, Yu Xing, Tong Chen, Nguyen Quoc Viet Hung, Hongzhi Yin
- **Abstract**: Large Language Model-based Time Series Forecasting (LLMTS) has shown remarkable promise in handling complex and diverse temporal data, representing a significant step toward foundation models for time series analysis. However, this emerging paradigm introduces two critical challenges. First, the substantial commercial potential and resource-intensive development raise urgent concerns about intellectual property (IP) protection. Second, their powerful time series forecasting capabilities may be misused to produce misleading or fabricated deepfake time series data. To address these concerns, we explore watermarking the outputs of LLMTS models, that is, embedding imperceptible signals into the generated time series data that remain detectable by specialized algorithms. We propose a novel post-hoc watermarking framework, Waltz, which is broadly compatible with existing LLMTS models. Waltz is inspired by the empirical observation that time series patch embeddings are rarely aligned with a specific set of LLM tokens, which we term ``cold tokens''. Leveraging this insight, Waltz embeds watermarks by rewiring the similarity statistics between patch embeddings and cold token embeddings, and detects watermarks using similarity z-scores. To minimize potential side effects, we introduce a similarity-based embedding position identification strategy and employ projected gradient descent to constrain the watermark noise within a defined boundary. Extensive experiments using two popular LLMTS models across seven benchmark datasets demonstrate that Waltz achieves high watermark detection accuracy with minimal impact on the quality of the generated time series.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the emerging need for intellectual property (IP) protection and misuse prevention in Large Language Model-based Time Series Forecasting (LLMTS) models.  It identifies two main risks: model distillation (unauthorized copying) and malicious generation of deepfake time series data. To address these concerns, the authors propose Waltz, a novel post-hoc watermarking framework applicable to a wide range of LLMTS models.  Waltz leverages the observation that time series patch embeddings are rarely aligned with specific LLM tokens ("cold tokens"). The framework embeds watermarks by rewiring the similarity statistics between patch and cold token embeddings, detecting watermarks using similarity z-scores, and minimizes side effects by similarity-based location identification and gradient descent to constrain watermark noise.  The authors evaluate Waltz on two LLMTS models and seven benchmark datasets, demonstrating high watermark detection accuracy with minimal impact on time series quality. They also show robustness against model distillation attacks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper is the first to specifically address the problem of watermarking LLMTS models in a generic, post-hoc manner.  While watermarking techniques are well-established in computer vision and NLP, their direct application to LLMTS is non-trivial due to the continuous nature of time series data and the specific architecture of LLMTS models.  The "cold token" approach is an interesting and non-obvious way to embed watermarks in this context. The combination of similarity-based location identification and projected gradient descent for noise control also contributes to the technical novelty.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the paper stems from the growing importance of LLMTS models in commercial and critical applications. Protecting these models from IP infringement and misuse is essential.  The paper's proposed framework addresses a real and emerging threat. The experimental results are comprehensive, demonstrating the effectiveness of Waltz across a variety of datasets and models. The distillation attack robustness is also a key contribution, highlighting the practicality of the framework.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the specific challenges of watermarking LLMTS models.
    *   **Technical Approach:** The "cold token" idea is novel and well-motivated. The framework is well-designed, combining multiple techniques for watermark embedding and detection.
    *   **Experimental Evaluation:** The experiments are comprehensive, covering multiple datasets, models, and attack scenarios.  The ablation study provides insights into the contribution of each component of the framework.
    *   **Post-hoc Design:** The post-hoc nature of the approach is a significant strength, as it makes it applicable to existing, pre-trained models.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Baseline Comparison:** While the authors compare against HTW (the only directly comparable work) and a few generic baselines, more comparisons to relevant, albeit non-identical, watermarking methods from related fields (e.g., methods designed for sequences or numerical data) could have strengthened the evaluation.
    *   **Real-World Deployment Considerations:** While the post-hoc design is a strength, the paper could benefit from discussing practical deployment considerations, such as the overhead of the watermark insertion and detection processes, and the computational cost of finding cold tokens (although this is a one-time process).
    *   **Threat Model Limitations:** The paper primarily focuses on model distillation. More discussion on other potential attacks, such as watermark removal through averaging multiple watermarked outputs or adversarial perturbations of the time series, would add to the robustness of the work.
*   **Potential Influence:** This paper is likely to be influential in the nascent field of watermarking for LLMTS models. It provides a solid foundation for future research and development in this area. It’s the first dedicated work on the topic that is well-executed.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper exhibits several strengths, particularly in its novelty, comprehensive experimental evaluation, and practical post-hoc design, there are some limitations. The baseline comparison could be more robust, and the discussion of real-world deployment and broader attack scenarios would improve the work. The idea of using cold tokens and re-aligning time series embeddings is new. Also, time series is different from watermarking images and texts. The approach considers the requirements of time series watermarking as a first step. Given these factors, a score of 7 is warranted.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Learning Only with Images: Visual Reinforcement Learning with Reasoning, Rendering, and Visual Feedback
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.20766v1)
- **Authors**: Yang Chen, Yufan Shen, Wenxuan Huang, Shen Zhou, Qunshu Lin, Xinyu Cai, Zhi Yu, Botian Shi, Yu Qiao
- **Abstract**: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have exhibited impressive performance across various visual tasks. Subsequent investigations into enhancing their visual reasoning abilities have significantly expanded their performance envelope. However, a critical bottleneck in the advancement of MLLMs toward deep visual reasoning is their heavy reliance on curated image-text supervision. To solve this problem, we introduce a novel framework termed ``Reasoning-Rendering-Visual-Feedback'' (RRVF), which enables MLLMs to learn complex visual reasoning from only raw images. This framework builds on the ``Asymmetry of Verification'' principle to train MLLMs, i.e., verifying the rendered output against a source image is easier than generating it. We demonstrate that this relative ease provides an ideal reward signal for optimization via Reinforcement Learning (RL) training, reducing the reliance on the image-text supervision. Guided by the above principle, RRVF implements a closed-loop iterative process encompassing reasoning, rendering, and visual feedback components, enabling the model to perform self-correction through multi-turn interactions and tool invocation, while this pipeline can be optimized by the GRPO algorithm in an end-to-end manner. Extensive experiments on image-to-code generation for data charts and web interfaces show that RRVF substantially outperforms existing open-source MLLMs and surpasses supervised fine-tuning baselines. Our findings demonstrate that systems driven by purely visual feedback present a viable path toward more robust and generalizable reasoning models without requiring explicit supervision. Code will be available at https://github.com/L-O-I/RRVF.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Learning Only with Images: Visual Reinforcement Learning with Reasoning, Rendering, and Visual Feedback":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel framework called Reasoning-Rendering-Visual-Feedback (RRVF) to train multimodal large language models (MLLMs) for complex visual reasoning tasks using only raw images. The core idea is to leverage the "Asymmetry of Verification" principle, where verifying the rendered output against the source image is easier than generating it from scratch. RRVF implements a closed-loop process involving reasoning, rendering executable code (e.g., HTML or chart generation code), and visual feedback based on the discrepancies between the rendered output and the original image.  The MLLM iteratively refines its code based on this visual feedback, optimizing the process via Reinforcement Learning with a hybrid reward function. Experiments on image-to-code generation for data charts and web interfaces demonstrate that RRVF outperforms existing open-source MLLMs and supervised fine-tuning baselines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces a genuinely novel approach to training MLLMs for visual reasoning by eliminating the need for paired image-text data. The RRVF framework, with its iterative reasoning, rendering, and visual feedback loop, is a creative solution to a significant bottleneck in MLLM development. It innovatively adapts the "Asymmetry of Verification" principle to the visual reasoning domain.
*   **Sound Methodology:** The approach is well-motivated by the "Asymmetry of Verification" principle and the success of RL in other areas of LLM training. The use of GRPO for optimization, along with the hybrid reward function, is well-justified. The experimental setup is rigorous and comprehensively benchmarks performance against a diverse range of baselines, including both open-source and closed-source models.
*   **Strong Results:** The empirical results are compelling. RRVF demonstrably outperforms existing open-source models and even surpasses supervised fine-tuning, showcasing the effectiveness of the approach. The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the contribution of each component of the framework. The result demonstrating that a smaller model (7B) trained with RRVF surpasses a much larger model (72B) used for visual judging/feedback is a particularly significant finding, suggesting strong knowledge distillation and efficient learning.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical limitation in MLLM development – the dependence on expensive and curated image-text datasets. By enabling learning from raw images, RRVF paves the way for more scalable and generalizable reasoning models. The framework also facilitates more interpretable reasoning by generating executable code.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Task Scope:** The current implementation of RRVF is limited to code reconstruction tasks. While these are complex tasks, the generalization to other visual reasoning tasks where explicit code generation might not be feasible or appropriate is unclear.  The method might not easily translate to tasks requiring abstract reasoning about visual concepts or understanding complex visual narratives.
*   **Dependency on the "Judge" Model:**  Although the paper demonstrates that the final trained model is not fundamentally limited by the "judge" model's capability, the framework is still heavily reliant on a capable visual judge for providing feedback. This introduces a dependence on the performance and biases of the judge model.
*   **Complexity:** The iterative reasoning, rendering, and feedback loop introduces significant computational complexity.  This might limit the scalability of the approach for training on very large datasets.

**Potential Impact:**

The RRVF framework has the potential to significantly impact the field of MLLMs by:

*   Reducing the dependence on expensive image-text datasets.
*   Enabling the development of more scalable and generalizable visual reasoning models.
*   Promoting more interpretable reasoning processes through code generation.
*   Opening up new avenues for self-supervised learning in visual domains.

**Justification for the Score:**

While the paper presents a strong contribution with novel ideas and solid results, the limited scope of the tasks prevents a higher score. There is also a significant dependency on the 'Judge' model that performs visual similarity checks. Therefore, the novelty of eliminating supervision is limited by reliance on another strong model. However, the framework addresses a significant problem in visual reasoning, the methodology is sound, and the results are impressive. The counter-intuitive result of the 7B model trained with feedback outperforming the 72B "teacher" model underscores the method's potential.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### evalSmarT: An LLM-Based Framework for Evaluating Smart Contract Generated Comments
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.20774v1)
- **Authors**: Fatou Ndiaye Mbodji
- **Abstract**: Smart contract comment generation has gained traction as a means to improve code comprehension and maintainability in blockchain systems. However, evaluating the quality of generated comments remains a challenge. Traditional metrics such as BLEU and ROUGE fail to capture domain-specific nuances, while human evaluation is costly and unscalable. In this paper, we present \texttt{evalSmarT}, a modular and extensible framework that leverages large language models (LLMs) as evaluators. The system supports over 400 evaluator configurations by combining approximately 40 LLMs with 10 prompting strategies. We demonstrate its application in benchmarking comment generation tools and selecting the most informative outputs. Our results show that prompt design significantly impacts alignment with human judgment, and that LLM-based evaluation offers a scalable and semantically rich alternative to existing methods.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces evalSmarT, a modular and extensible framework that uses Large Language Models (LLMs) to evaluate the quality of automatically generated comments for smart contracts. The framework addresses the limitations of traditional evaluation metrics (BLEU, ROUGE) that fail to capture domain-specific nuances and the scalability issues of human evaluation. evalSmarT supports a wide range of evaluator configurations by combining multiple LLMs with various prompting strategies designed to incorporate domain knowledge, language features, and evaluation framing.  The paper demonstrates the framework's application in benchmarking comment generation tools and selecting the most informative outputs. The results highlight the importance of prompt design and suggest that LLM-based evaluation offers a scalable and semantically rich alternative to existing methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Addresses a clear gap:** The paper correctly identifies the limitations of existing evaluation methods for smart contract comments. Traditional metrics are inadequate, and human evaluation is impractical at scale.
*   **Novel approach:** Utilizing LLMs as evaluators is a promising direction. While LLMs have been used for code generation and summarization, their use as evaluators, especially with domain-specific prompts, is a valuable contribution.
*   **Modular and Extensible Design:** The modularity of evalSmarT is a significant strength. It allows for easy integration of new LLMs and prompt strategies, making the framework adaptable to future advancements.
*   **Systematic Evaluation:** The paper demonstrates a systematic approach to evaluating LLM-based evaluators, highlighting the impact of different prompt designs.
*   **Demonstration and Use Cases:** The demonstration comparing SCCLLM and CCGIR provides a practical example of how evalSmarT can be used to benchmark and analyze comment generation tools.
*   **Code and Data Availability:** The provision of code and data promotes reproducibility and further research.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope of Comparison:** While the paper compares SCCLLM and CCGIR, a more comprehensive benchmark involving a wider range of comment generation tools would strengthen the findings.
*   **Dependence on LLM Quality:** The framework's performance heavily relies on the capabilities of the underlying LLMs. While GPT-4 is used, the performance of other LLMs might vary significantly. The paper acknowledges this but could explore the limitations of less capable LLMs in more detail.
*   **Potential Bias:** The LLM evaluators might be biased based on their training data. This potential bias and its impact on the evaluation results are not thoroughly discussed.
*   **Generalizability:** The experiments are conducted within the context of Solidity smart contracts. The generalizability of the framework to other programming languages and domains is not explicitly addressed.
*   **Subjectivity of Prompt Design:** The effectiveness of evalSmarT is highly dependent on the design of the prompts. While the paper explores different prompting strategies, the selection and design of these prompts can be subjective. It would be beneficial to have a clearer methodology for prompt engineering.
*   **Limited Human Evaluation Comparison:** While the paper mentions alignment with human judgment, a direct comparison of evalSmarT's evaluations with extensive human evaluations could be useful to validate how well LLMs can really emulate human evaluation quality.

**Significance:**

The paper's contribution lies in providing a practical framework for evaluating smart contract comments using LLMs, filling a gap in the existing literature. It highlights the importance of prompt design and demonstrates the potential of LLM-based evaluation as a scalable and semantically rich alternative to traditional methods.  The framework can aid researchers and practitioners in developing and evaluating better comment generation tools for smart contracts, which is important for improving code comprehension and maintainability in blockchain systems.

**Overall Justification:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of smart contract analysis and code understanding. While there are some limitations, the strengths of the paper outweigh the weaknesses. The idea of using LLMs as evaluators, the modular design of the framework, and the systematic evaluation are all significant contributions. However, the dependence on LLM quality and prompt engineering, as well as the need for more extensive benchmarking and validation, keep it from being a truly exceptional work.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Automating Thematic Review of Prevention of Future Deaths Reports: Replicating the ONS Child Suicide Study using Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.20786v1)
- **Authors**: Sam Osian, Arpan Dutta, Sahil Bhandari, Iain E. Buchan, Dan W. Joyce
- **Abstract**: Prevention of Future Deaths (PFD) reports, issued by coroners in England and Wales, flag systemic hazards that may lead to further loss of life. Analysis of these reports has previously been constrained by the manual effort required to identify and code relevant cases. In 2025, the Office for National Statistics (ONS) published a national thematic review of child-suicide PFD reports ($\leq$ 18 years), identifying 37 cases from January 2015 to November 2023 - a process based entirely on manual curation and coding. We evaluated whether a fully automated, open source "text-to-table" language-model pipeline (PFD Toolkit) could reproduce the ONS's identification and thematic analysis of child-suicide PFD reports, and assessed gains in efficiency and reliability. All 4,249 PFD reports published from July 2013 to November 2023 were processed via PFD Toolkit's large language model pipelines. Automated screening identified cases where the coroner attributed death to suicide in individuals aged 18 or younger, and eligible reports were coded for recipient category and 23 concern sub-themes, replicating the ONS coding frame. PFD Toolkit identified 72 child-suicide PFD reports - almost twice the ONS count. Three blinded clinicians adjudicated a stratified sample of 144 reports to validate the child-suicide screening. Against the post-consensus clinical annotations, the LLM-based workflow showed substantial to almost-perfect agreement (Cohen's $\kappa$ = 0.82, 95% CI: 0.66-0.98, raw agreement = 91%). The end-to-end script runtime was 8m 16s, transforming a process that previously took months into one that can be completed in minutes. This demonstrates that automated LLM analysis can reliably and efficiently replicate manual thematic reviews of coronial data, enabling scalable, reproducible, and timely insights for public health and safety. The PFD Toolkit is openly available for future research.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a method for automating the thematic review of Prevention of Future Deaths (PFD) reports, which are statutory notices issued by coroners in England and Wales highlighting potential public safety hazards.  The authors used their open-source "PFD Toolkit," powered by large language models (LLMs), to replicate a manual review conducted by the Office for National Statistics (ONS) on child-suicide PFD reports.  The Toolkit successfully identified child-suicide PFD reports, coded them for recipient category and concern sub-themes, and benchmarked its performance against clinical annotations.  The results indicate that the LLM-based pipeline can efficiently and reliably replicate manual thematic reviews, reducing processing time from months to minutes. The authors argue that their method enables scalable, reproducible, and timely insights for public health and safety research.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the application of a *fully* automated LLM-driven pipeline to process and analyze PFD reports, including OCR on scanned documents. While individual components (LLMs, OCR, web scraping) are not novel in themselves, their integration into an end-to-end system for coronial data analysis represents a useful contribution. The claim of being able to reproduce a process that took months to minutes is a fairly big claim, and if correct, then is significant. The open-source nature of the PFD Toolkit further enhances its accessibility and potential for impact. The use of a domain-specific application and its comparison to the national statistics office for a particular thematic investigation is a solid evaluation. The identification of missed cases through this methodology is also valuable.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from addressing a recognized problem: the under-exploitation of PFD reports due to the manual effort required for analysis. The automated approach proposed in this paper could significantly enhance public safety and research by enabling rapid identification of systemic issues contributing to preventable deaths. The comparison to the ONS study is crucial, showing that the LLM pipeline can achieve comparable (or even better) results with significantly less human effort. There are some limitations when directly comparing results, due to different ways of counting data points. The high agreement between the Toolkit output and clinical adjudication lends credibility to the automated approach, indicating a viable method for research, audit, and policy development.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Open-source and readily accessible Toolkit.
    *   End-to-end automation, addressing the entire workflow.
    *   Comprehensive comparison to a real-world manual study (ONS).
    *   High agreement with clinical experts.
    *   Significant reduction in processing time.
    *   Identifies missed data points that the ONS didn't manage to extract, and which would have taken them more resources/time to get there.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Dependence on OpenAI's GPT-4.1: The study acknowledges that the performance depends on the specific LLM used, meaning future iterations of GPT-4 and alternative models could provide different results. While the Toolkit allows for local LLM frameworks, the benchmark study focuses on a proprietary, cloud-based service. This may limit its accessibility and replicability in contexts with limited resources or data privacy concerns.
    *   Data Quality Considerations: The reliance on the quality and completeness of the PFD reports themselves is also a limitation. As the authors note, age information is not always recorded, potentially leading to missed cases. The method is also limited by the Chief Coroner's redaction of sensitive content.
    * Limited Comparison to ONS Data: There's an acknowledgment that a direct comparison to the ONS bulletin is not perfect, because they count their numbers differently, which weakens the claims a little.
    * No in-depth assessment of the extracted themes: The themes that are extracted are not deeply analyzed, which is a little disappointing.

**Justification of Score:**

While the paper demonstrates a promising and efficient approach for analyzing PFD reports, the dependence on a proprietary LLM and the lack of a more detailed analysis of the findings temper the overall assessment. The PFD Toolkit offers a valuable contribution by providing an efficient and scalable means of conducting thematic reviews of PFD reports. While there are acknowledged limitations, the strengths of the approach and its potential to improve public safety research warrant a strong score.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### FantasyID: A dataset for detecting digital manipulations of ID-documents
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.20808v1)
- **Authors**: Pavel Korshunov, Amir Mohammadi, Vidit Vidit, Christophe Ecabert, Sébastien Marcel
- **Abstract**: Advancements in image generation led to the availability of easy-to-use tools for malicious actors to create forged images. These tools pose a serious threat to the widespread Know Your Customer (KYC) applications, requiring robust systems for detection of the forged Identity Documents (IDs). To facilitate the development of the detection algorithms, in this paper, we propose a novel publicly available (including commercial use) dataset, FantasyID, which mimics real-world IDs but without tampering with legal documents and, compared to previous public datasets, it does not contain generated faces or specimen watermarks. FantasyID contains ID cards with diverse design styles, languages, and faces of real people. To simulate a realistic KYC scenario, the cards from FantasyID were printed and captured with three different devices, constituting the bonafide class. We have emulated digital forgery/injection attacks that could be performed by a malicious actor to tamper the IDs using the existing generative tools. The current state-of-the-art forgery detection algorithms, such as TruFor, MMFusion, UniFD, and FatFormer, are challenged by FantasyID dataset. It especially evident, in the evaluation conditions close to practical, with the operational threshold set on validation set so that false positive rate is at 10%, leading to false negative rates close to 50% across the board on the test set. The evaluation experiments demonstrate that FantasyID dataset is complex enough to be used as an evaluation benchmark for detection algorithms.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces FantasyID, a novel, publicly available dataset designed to facilitate research in detecting digital manipulations of ID documents. Unlike existing datasets, FantasyID consists of pristine, bonafide ID card images alongside digitally forged versions, mitigating biases present in datasets that use tampered versions of official documents. The dataset includes ID cards with diverse designs, languages, and faces of real people. The authors simulate realistic KYC scenarios by printing the cards and capturing them with different devices. They also emulate digital forgery attacks using generative tools.  The paper evaluates state-of-the-art forgery detection algorithms (TruFor, MMFusion, UniFD, and FatFormer) on FantasyID, demonstrating the dataset's challenge, especially in realistic operational conditions.  The results show that existing algorithms struggle, with high false negative rates even when false positive rates are constrained.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the dataset itself. The paper explicitly addresses a gap in the availability of high-quality, publicly accessible datasets for ID document forgery detection.  Previous datasets often suffer from issues such as:
    *   Tampered bonafide images (e.g., removing "specimen" markings).
    *   Limited diversity in languages and designs.
    *   Use of generated faces for bonafide images.
    *   Lack of commercial use allowance.

    FantasyID addresses these limitations by providing pristine bonafide cards with diverse designs, real faces, and a commercially permissive license. The inclusion of different types of digital manipulations (face swapping, text inpainting) is also a positive aspect. The detailed documentation of how the dataset was created is valuable for reproducibility.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this work is in providing a challenging and realistic benchmark for ID document forgery detection algorithms. With the increasing sophistication of image generation techniques and the prevalence of online KYC processes, the ability to detect forged ID documents is crucial. By demonstrating the limitations of existing algorithms on FantasyID, the authors highlight the need for further research in this area. The dataset has the potential to drive progress by enabling fair comparisons of different approaches and encouraging the development of more robust detection methods.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Dataset Design:** FantasyID includes a wide range of languages, card styles, and attack types, making it a more representative and challenging benchmark compared to existing datasets. The inclusion of both digital originals and physically printed/recaptured versions is a strength.
    *   **Realistic Scenarios:** Simulating KYC processes and digital manipulation attacks increases the practical relevance of the dataset.
    *   **Public Availability:** The availability under a permissive license fosters collaboration and accelerates research in the field.
    *   **Rigorous Evaluation:** The evaluation of state-of-the-art algorithms provides a clear baseline and demonstrates the dataset's difficulty.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope of Attacks:** The dataset focuses on digital injection/manipulation attacks. Other forms of forgery, such as physical alterations of the ID document or presentation attacks (e.g., using a printed fake ID presented to a camera), are not considered. While this focus is justified, it represents a limitation in the overall scope.
    *   **Dataset Size:** While an improvement over prior datasets, the size (362 bonafide, with derived attack sets) could still be considered modest.  Larger datasets often lead to better generalization performance of machine learning models.
    *   **Performance of evaluated methods:** While valuable in demonstrating the challenge posed by FantasyID, the consistent poor performance of all four assessed methods hints at a possible methodological weakness in the evaluation or an inherent design limitation of the dataset that particularly challenges current models. This point warrants further investigation.

* **Score Justification:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field by providing a novel and challenging dataset for ID document forgery detection. The strengths of the dataset design, realistic scenarios, public availability, and rigorous evaluation outweigh the limitations in scope and size. However, the limited diversity of attacks and the rather moderate size of the dataset keep the score from being higher. Also the surprisingly poor results suggest that the evaluation protocols should be scrutinized. Therefore, I assign a score of **7**. The FantasyID dataset is a significant step forward in enabling research in this critical area, but it is not without its limitations. Future work could focus on expanding the dataset to include more diverse attacks, increasing the dataset size, and exploring localization performance rather than merely binary decision. The inclusion of results from robust evaluation protocols are warranted.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Latent Inter-User Difference Modeling for LLM Personalization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.20849v1)
- **Authors**: Yilun Qiu, Tianhao Shi, Xiaoyan Zhao, Fengbin Zhu, Yang Zhang, Fuli Feng
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly integrated into users' daily lives, leading to a growing demand for personalized outputs. Previous work focuses on leveraging a user's own history, overlooking inter-user differences that are crucial for effective personalization. While recent work has attempted to model such differences, the reliance on language-based prompts often hampers the effective extraction of meaningful distinctions. To address these issues, we propose Difference-aware Embedding-based Personalization (DEP), a framework that models inter-user differences in the latent space instead of relying on language prompts. DEP constructs soft prompts by contrasting a user's embedding with those of peers who engaged with similar content, highlighting relative behavioral signals. A sparse autoencoder then filters and compresses both user-specific and difference-aware embeddings, preserving only task-relevant features before injecting them into a frozen LLM. Experiments on personalized review generation show that DEP consistently outperforms baseline methods across multiple metrics. Our code is available at https://github.com/SnowCharmQ/DEP.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes "Difference-aware Embedding-based Personalization" (DEP), a novel approach to LLM personalization that focuses on modeling inter-user differences in the latent embedding space rather than relying on language-based prompts.  DEP extracts a user-specific embedding and a difference-aware embedding (based on contrasting the user's embedding with those of peers who interacted with similar content).  These embeddings are then distilled using a sparse autoencoder (SAE) to retain only task-relevant features and injected into a frozen LLM as soft prompts. Experiments on personalized review generation demonstrate that DEP outperforms existing methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The central idea of modeling inter-user *differences* in a *latent space* for LLM personalization has merit and is a relative strength. Prior work, including the DPL method that this paper builds upon, leverages natural language prompts, which can be cumbersome and may not effectively capture nuanced distinctions. The use of a sparse autoencoder to filter and compress the embeddings is a standard technique for feature selection and noise reduction, but its application in this specific personalization context adds incremental value. The injection of these latent representations as soft prompts is a common practice in LLM adaptation, but is appropriate in this context.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance is moderate. Personalization is a crucial area for LLMs, and the paper contributes to a growing body of research focused on improving the ability of LLMs to adapt to individual users. Showing performance improvements on review generation, a widely-used benchmark, is valuable.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The move to latent-space modeling addresses limitations of language-based prompt engineering for capturing inter-user differences.
    *   The experimental results consistently demonstrate DEP's superior performance compared to several baselines, indicating its effectiveness.
    *   The ablation studies provide insights into the contribution of different components of DEP, validating the design choices.
    *   The paper is well-written and clearly presents the proposed method and experimental results.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The novelty, while present, is not groundbreaking. Most components are known techniques in the literature. The main contribution lies in the specific combination and application to the LLM personalization task.
    *   The reliance on user history for generating embeddings can be problematic in cold-start scenarios (when a user has limited interaction data). The paper acknowledges this limitation, but more discussion about how to mitigate this in practice would be beneficial.
    *   While the experimental results are compelling, the evaluation is limited to review generation. Generalizability to other tasks, such as dialogue or recommendation, remains an open question.
    *   The computational overhead of creating difference-aware embeddings and training the SAE is a consideration. It is unclear how DEP scales to very large user bases and diverse content.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research in LLM personalization by highlighting the benefits of modeling inter-user differences in the latent space. It provides a concrete framework for achieving this, which can be adapted and extended in various ways. The use of sparse autoencoders for feature selection in this context could be further explored in other applications.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the above analysis, I assign the paper a score of 6.5. The paper makes a solid, incremental contribution to the field of LLM personalization. While the individual components of DEP are not entirely novel, their combination and application to the task of capturing inter-user differences in the latent space is well-motivated and results in demonstrated performance improvements. The weaknesses related to cold-start scenarios, limited task evaluation, and computational overhead prevent a higher score.

**Score: 6.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Compositional Video Synthesis by Temporal Object-Centric Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.20855v1)
- **Authors**: Adil Kaan Akan, Yucel Yemez
- **Abstract**: We present a novel framework for compositional video synthesis that leverages temporally consistent object-centric representations, extending our previous work, SlotAdapt, from images to video. While existing object-centric approaches either lack generative capabilities entirely or treat video sequences holistically, thus neglecting explicit object-level structure, our approach explicitly captures temporal dynamics by learning pose invariant object-centric slots and conditioning them on pretrained diffusion models. This design enables high-quality, pixel-level video synthesis with superior temporal coherence, and offers intuitive compositional editing capabilities such as object insertion, deletion, or replacement, maintaining consistent object identities across frames. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our method sets new benchmarks in video generation quality and temporal consistency, outperforming previous object-centric generative methods. Although our segmentation performance closely matches state-of-the-art methods, our approach uniquely integrates this capability with robust generative performance, significantly advancing interactive and controllable video generation and opening new possibilities for advanced content creation, semantic editing, and dynamic scene understanding.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Compositional Video Synthesis by Temporal Object-Centric Learning":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel framework for compositional video synthesis that extends the authors' previous work, SlotAdapt, from images to video.  The approach learns temporally consistent object-centric representations (slots) conditioned on pretrained diffusion models.  This enables high-quality, pixel-level video synthesis with superior temporal coherence compared to other object-centric methods.  The framework also provides intuitive compositional editing capabilities like object insertion, deletion, and replacement while maintaining consistent object identities across frames. The approach is self-supervised and tested on real-world datasets.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** While the individual components (Slot Attention, diffusion models, use of DINO features) are not new, the *combination* of these components in a *temporal* and *object-centric* video generation framework is the primary source of novelty. Specifically, the authors present a novel method for maintaining temporal consistency of object-centric slots in a self-supervised manner, which is significant, as many previous approaches require external signals like optical flow. A key contribution is also extending compositional editing to real-world videos in a way that maintains both temporal coherence and semantic consistency. The use of adapter layers to align slot semantics with a pre-trained diffusion model appears to be an effective design choice. However, the paper builds significantly on the authors' previous work (SlotAdapt), and the core architectural ideas share similarities with existing OCL methods. While the extension to video is important, it doesn't represent a complete paradigm shift.

**Significance:** The paper is significant because it addresses a critical gap in the field: controllable and compositional video generation with object-level understanding. Current text-to-video models lack object-level control, while previous object-centric methods either lack generative capabilities or treat video holistically. The paper's framework opens possibilities for:

*   Advanced content creation and semantic video editing.
*   Dynamic scene understanding.
*   Potentially, building video models capable of reasoning and interacting with dynamic environments.

However, the reliance on pretrained diffusion models and DINO features may limit the generalizability and adaptability of the approach to new domains. Also, the "1-frame training strategy" used to reduce computational costs raises questions about the method's performance on tasks requiring explicit long-term temporal dependencies. The qualitative results provided are convincing, but a more detailed analysis of the failure cases and limitations would strengthen the paper. Finally, comparing with text-to-video models (even if these models lack object-centric understanding) would highlight the benefits and drawbacks of the compositional nature of the method.

**Strengths:**

*   Addresses a gap in the field of video generation.
*   Enables compositional video editing in real-world scenarios.
*   Combines object-centric representation learning with high-quality synthesis.
*   Strong qualitative results.

**Weaknesses:**

*   Builds heavily on the authors' previous work (SlotAdapt).
*   Relies on pretrained diffusion models and DINO features which may limit generalizability.
*   The 1-frame training strategy could limit performance on long-term temporal dependencies.
*   Lack of comparisons against the latest text-to-video models.
*   More detailed analysis of limitations/failure cases is needed.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a meaningful contribution to the field of video synthesis by combining object-centric representation learning with powerful diffusion models. The ability to perform compositional editing in real-world videos with temporal coherence is significant. While the framework relies heavily on existing components and the improvement is more of an incremental advance, the experimental results are convincing and clearly show better object-centric generation performance. Taking into account both the novelty and significance, and the stated limitations, a score of **7** is appropriate. The paper effectively leverages existing techniques to solve a challenging problem with clear practical applications, but it needs further refinement to address the generalizability and scalability concerns.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Leveraging Open-Source Large Language Models for Clinical Information Extraction in Resource-Constrained Settings
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.20859v1)
- **Authors**: Luc Builtjes, Joeran Bosma, Mathias Prokop, Bram van Ginneken, Alessa Hering
- **Abstract**: Medical reports contain rich clinical information but are often unstructured and written in domain-specific language, posing challenges for information extraction. While proprietary large language models (LLMs) have shown promise in clinical natural language processing, their lack of transparency and data privacy concerns limit their utility in healthcare. This study therefore evaluates nine open-source generative LLMs on the DRAGON benchmark, which includes 28 clinical information extraction tasks in Dutch. We developed \texttt{llm\_extractinator}, a publicly available framework for information extraction using open-source generative LLMs, and used it to assess model performance in a zero-shot setting. Several 14 billion parameter models, Phi-4-14B, Qwen-2.5-14B, and DeepSeek-R1-14B, achieved competitive results, while the bigger Llama-3.3-70B model achieved slightly higher performance at greater computational cost. Translation to English prior to inference consistently degraded performance, highlighting the need of native-language processing. These findings demonstrate that open-source LLMs, when used with our framework, offer effective, scalable, and privacy-conscious solutions for clinical information extraction in low-resource settings.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Leveraging Open-Source Large Language Models for Clinical Information Extraction in Resource-Constrained Settings":

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the applicability of open-source Large Language Models (LLMs) for clinical information extraction (IE) from Dutch medical reports, addressing the limitations of proprietary LLMs concerning transparency and data privacy in healthcare. The authors introduce "llm_extractinator," a framework designed to facilitate the use of these models for IE tasks, and evaluate nine open-source LLMs on the DRAGON benchmark, a dataset of Dutch medical reports. The study assesses the models' zero-shot performance, explores the impact of translating Dutch text to English before inference, and analyzes the strengths and weaknesses of different model architectures for specific IE tasks. The key findings demonstrate the potential of open-source LLMs, particularly 14B parameter models like Phi-4, Qwen-2.5, and DeepSeek-R1, for achieving competitive results in resource-constrained settings, while highlighting the detrimental effect of translation and emphasizing the need for native-language processing.

**Rigorous and Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper contributes a valuable, though somewhat incremental, step forward in the application of LLMs in clinical NLP. The novelty lies in:

    *   *Focus on Open-Source LLMs in a Resource-Constrained Language:*  Many studies focus on proprietary models and English. This work specifically addresses open-source models and the challenges of a language with fewer resources (Dutch).
    *   *Systematic Evaluation and Framework:* The llm_extractinator framework provides a useful tool for the community and facilitates the evaluation of different LLMs. The systematic approach to evaluating multiple models on the DRAGON benchmark adds to the reliability of the results.
    *   *Negative Result on Translation:* The clear demonstration that translation harms performance is valuable and counterintuitive, adding to the body of knowledge regarding cross-lingual transfer.

    However, the core ideas (using LLMs for IE) are not fundamentally new.  It's an application of existing techniques to a specific domain and language.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from the practical implications:

    *   *Privacy-Conscious Healthcare AI:* The study showcases how open-source LLMs can be used in healthcare while adhering to privacy regulations by enabling local processing of sensitive data.
    *   *Scalable Solutions:* The llm_extractinator framework enables scalable information extraction from medical reports, automating tasks previously requiring manual annotation or rule-based systems.
    *   *Reduced Dependence on Large, Proprietary Models:*  The paper helps identify smaller, more computationally efficient open-source LLMs that can perform comparably to larger models in certain tasks.

    The limitations are:

    *   *Zero-Shot Setting:* The study is limited to zero-shot performance. While this demonstrates generalizability, the models might achieve significantly better results with fine-tuning, few-shot learning, or lightweight instruction tuning.
