    *   *Limited Scope of DRAGON Benchmark:*  The DRAGON benchmark, while valuable, is limited to Dutch and specific task definitions. Generalizability to other languages and clinical scenarios needs further investigation.
    *   *Lack of in-depth error analysis:* While useful, there is limited analysis on types of errors, and some errors seem to come from specific LLMs rather than all evaluated LLMs.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Well-defined research question and methodology.
    *   Clear presentation of results and insightful discussion.
    *   Publicly available framework (llm_extractinator) promotes reproducibility and further research.
    *   Systematic evaluation of multiple models.
    *   Negative control with the in-context translation experiments.
    *   Well-motivated, in relation to real-world problems.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Focus on zero-shot learning only.
    *   Limited dataset (Dutch medical reports).
    *   Lack of a more thorough error analysis.
    *   Limited scope - Only a few models were compared directly in the machine translation experiments.
    *   Some of the conclusions regarding token generation seem a bit obvious

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper can influence the field by encouraging more research into open-source LLMs for clinical NLP, providing a framework for future evaluations, and highlighting the importance of native-language processing.  It could also inform the development of more privacy-preserving and scalable AI solutions for healthcare.

*   **Justification:** The novelty and significance are somewhat limited by the incremental nature of the research and the scope of the dataset. However, the focus on open-source LLMs, the practical implications for healthcare, and the systematic evaluation methodology justify a moderate score.
    Score: 6.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Enhancing Project-Specific Code Completion by Inferring Internal API Information
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.20888v1)
- **Authors**: Le Deng, Xiaoxue Ren, Chao Ni, Ming Liang, David Lo, Zhongxin Liu
- **Abstract**: Project-specific code completion is a critical task that leverages context from a project to generate accurate code. State-of-the-art methods use retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) with large language models (LLMs) and project information for code completion. However, they often struggle to incorporate internal API information, which is crucial for accuracy, especially when APIs are not explicitly imported in the file. To address this, we propose a method to infer internal API information without relying on imports. Our method extends the representation of APIs by constructing usage examples and semantic descriptions, building a knowledge base for LLMs to generate relevant completions. We also introduce ProjBench, a benchmark that avoids leaked imports and consists of large-scale real-world projects. Experiments on ProjBench and CrossCodeEval show that our approach significantly outperforms existing methods, improving code exact match by 22.72% and identifier exact match by 18.31%. Additionally, integrating our method with existing baselines boosts code match by 47.80% and identifier match by 35.55%.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of project-specific code completion, focusing on the limitations of existing retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) approaches in capturing internal API information.  Existing methods either retrieve similar code snippets that may not include relevant APIs or rely on import statements, which are often absent during early stages of development. The authors propose a new method that infers internal API information by: 1) constructing usage examples and functional semantic descriptions for each API in the project; 2) using a code draft generated by an LLM to infer necessary API information for the completion task; and 3) retrieving relevant APIs based on both usage examples and functional semantics.  They introduce a new benchmark, ProjBench, designed to avoid data leakage and misalignment with typical usage patterns, and demonstrate improved performance over baseline methods on both ProjBench and the CrossCodeEval benchmark.  The paper also shows that the proposed API inference method can be integrated into existing code completion frameworks to improve their performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its API inference method. The idea of using a code draft (an imperfect but informative initial completion) to guide the retrieval of relevant internal APIs is a clever approach that overcomes the limitations of relying solely on similarity-based retrieval or explicit import statements. The explicit construction of usage examples and functional semantic descriptions for APIs is also a significant contribution. This differentiates it from previous work focusing mostly on code snippet retrieval. This targeted construction for internal API knowledge also helps make the retrieval efficient by focusing on information key to the completion task.

* **Significance:** The problem of capturing project-specific knowledge for code completion is a practical and important one. The paper's results clearly demonstrate the effectiveness of their API inference method in improving code completion accuracy and relevance. The construction of the ProjBench benchmark is a valuable contribution as it addresses the limitations of existing benchmarks and better reflects real-world development scenarios, particularly by avoiding data leakage and considering the absence of import statements. Also, the framework's flexibility allows easy extension for use with new LLMs as they evolve.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Well-defined problem and solution:** The paper clearly identifies a critical gap in existing code completion approaches and presents a well-motivated and technically sound solution.
    * **Comprehensive evaluation:** The evaluation is thorough, using multiple benchmarks, multiple models, and various ablation studies to demonstrate the effectiveness and importance of each component of the proposed method.
    * **Practical focus:**  The focus on practical code completion scenarios (i.e. no import statements), realistic projects and ease of integration into existing systems is a strong point.
    * **New Benchmark:** Introduction of ProjBench is very valuable to the community.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Reliance on LLMs for docstring generation:** The paper uses LLMs to generate docstrings for API summarization. The quality of these docstrings significantly affects the accuracy of API retrieval.  While the authors use in-context learning to improve docstring quality, the potential for inaccurate or incomplete docstrings could still limit the performance of the method. The details behind the selection of "good" examples are not explicit and may require significant engineering effort to identify good code-docstring pairs.
    * **Heuristic-based usage example generation:**  The usage example generation relies on a set of heuristics, which might not cover all possible usage patterns. This could limit the retrieval of relevant API information in certain cases. While they cover the key cases, the selection is not based on empirical data on real world API call patterns.
    * **Limited Semantic Understanding:** The functional semantic retrieval is heavily reliant on docstring similarity, potentially missing nuanced semantic connections.
    * **Time Consumption:** While the paper states the offline construction is practical, the construction and inference time costs must be justified in more scenarios, especially when applied to very large codebases.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the field of project-specific code completion. The API inference method offers a practical and effective way to capture and leverage internal API information, which is crucial for accurate and relevant code completion. The ProjBench benchmark will likely become a valuable resource for evaluating future code completion approaches.

**Justification for Score:**

I'm assigning a score of **8**. The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of project-specific code completion. The API inference method addresses a key limitation of existing approaches, and the comprehensive evaluation demonstrates its effectiveness. The construction of the ProjBench benchmark is also a valuable contribution. While there are some weaknesses related to the reliance on LLMs for docstring generation and the heuristic-based nature of usage example generation, these limitations are relatively minor and do not detract significantly from the overall quality and impact of the paper. The potential to improve existing solutions through simple integration also increases the practical applicability of the solution. The paper demonstrates significant insight and well-executed experimentation, and I expect it to be very influential in the field.
Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 8/10

### Soft Injection of Task Embeddings Outperforms Prompt-Based In-Context Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.20906v1)
- **Authors**: Jungwon Park, Wonjong Rhee
- **Abstract**: In-Context Learning (ICL) enables Large Language Models (LLMs) to perform tasks by conditioning on input-output examples in the prompt, without requiring any update in model parameters. While widely adopted, it remains unclear whether prompting with multiple examples is the most effective and efficient way to convey task information. In this work, we propose Soft Injection of task embeddings. The task embeddings are constructed only once using few-shot ICL prompts and repeatedly used during inference. Soft injection is performed by softly mixing task embeddings with attention head activations using pre-optimized mixing parameters, referred to as soft head-selection parameters. This method not only allows a desired task to be performed without in-prompt demonstrations but also significantly outperforms existing ICL approaches while reducing memory usage and compute cost at inference time. An extensive evaluation is performed across 57 tasks and 12 LLMs, spanning four model families of sizes from 4B to 70B. Averaged across 57 tasks, our method outperforms 10-shot ICL by 10.1%-13.9% across 12 LLMs. Additional analyses show that our method also serves as an insightful tool for analyzing task-relevant roles of attention heads, revealing that task-relevant head positions selected by our method transfer across similar tasks but not across dissimilar ones -- underscoring the task-specific nature of head functionality. Our soft injection method opens a new paradigm for reducing prompt length and improving task performance by shifting task conditioning from the prompt space to the activation space.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "SITE" (Soft Injection of Task Embeddings), a novel approach to in-context learning (ICL) that replaces in-prompt demonstrations with softly injected task embeddings into attention head activations.  These task embeddings are created by averaging attention head activations from a few-shot ICL prompt.  The injection is controlled by learned "soft head-selection parameters" optimized via gradient descent.  The method outperforms existing ICL approaches, reduces memory usage and compute costs during inference, and provides insights into the task-relevant roles of attention heads.  The authors perform an extensive evaluation across 57 ICL tasks and 12 LLMs. The results demonstrate a significant performance improvement over 10-shot ICL, and that task embeddings and injection parameters generalize across similar tasks but not dissimilar ones.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of replacing in-prompt demonstrations with task embeddings is not entirely new (e.g., Function Vectors). However, the specific approach of *softly* injecting these embeddings into attention head activations, controlled by *learned* head-selection parameters optimized via gradient descent, is a significant advancement. The continuous optimization aspect, contrasting with hard selection methods, is a key innovation. The soft blending of the original activations adds a subtle, but useful nuance.

*   **Significance:** The potential impact of this work is substantial. First, the significant performance gains compared to standard ICL, especially in zero-shot inference settings, are valuable. Secondly, the reduction in inference time memory and computational costs makes LLMs more accessible and efficient to use for many tasks. Finally, the method offers a new lens for analyzing attention head functionality and task-specific roles, furthering mechanistic interpretability research. The demonstration that task embedding provides an insight into which heads are important and that these head positions transfer for related tasks is an excellent contribution.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Extensive Evaluation:** The authors conduct a thorough evaluation across a large number of tasks and models, lending strong support to their claims.
    *   **Clear Methodology:** The paper clearly explains the SITE method, including the optimization process and the injection mechanism.
    *   **Insightful Analyses:**  The analyses exploring attention head roles and cross-task transferability are valuable contributions.
    *   **Efficiency:** The demonstrated improvements in memory and computational efficiency make SITE a practically appealing alternative to standard ICL.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Dependence on Few-Shot Prompts for Embedding Creation:** The method still relies on few-shot ICL prompts to create task embeddings, although only once. A truly zero-shot (i.e., no example-dependent information is needed to create the task embedding) or unsupervised approach for constructing the embeddings would be even more impactful. Although they have a robustness experiment on the number of prompts to create the embeddings, further experiments on more complex prompts could improve the effectiveness of the task embeddings.
    *   **Hyperparameter Tuning:** Although the authors intentionally avoided task/model-specific hyperparameter tuning to highlight generality, carefully tuning parameters like learning rates and training iterations *could* further boost performance.
    *   **Limited Comparison to Other Injection Methods:** The comparisons to Function Vectors and MTV are limited, focusing only on Llama-3.1-8B. More extensive comparative analyses would strengthen the claims of superiority.

*   **Potential Influence:** The work has strong potential to influence both practical LLM deployment and interpretability research. It offers a practical way to reduce prompt length, improve performance, and offers a tool to analyze heads. It also demonstrates there are better ways to use task information than just showing the LLM many examples in-context.

*   **Rigorous Rationale:** While the basic idea of task embeddings has been explored, the key novelty lies in the `soft injection` and the continuous optimization of attention head selection. The thorough experimental evaluation validates the benefit of this method. The gains in both performance and efficiency suggest the work has merit, yet it requires further exploration with other task embedding creation strategies.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:** The paper makes a significant and validated contribution to in-context learning. While it builds upon existing concepts of task embeddings, the core innovation of *softly injecting with gradient descent* and the focus on *efficiency and analysis* are well substantiated and have significant practical implications. It loses marks on the dependence on initial few-shot prompts for embedding creation and a limited comparative analysis with other competing injection approaches.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### MediQAl: A French Medical Question Answering Dataset for Knowledge and Reasoning Evaluation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.20917v1)
- **Authors**: Adrien Bazoge
- **Abstract**: This work introduces MediQAl, a French medical question answering dataset designed to evaluate the capabilities of language models in factual medical recall and reasoning over real-world clinical scenarios. MediQAl contains 32,603 questions sourced from French medical examinations across 41 medical subjects. The dataset includes three tasks: (i) Multiple-Choice Question with Unique answer, (ii) Multiple-Choice Question with Multiple answer, and (iii) Open-Ended Question with Short-Answer. Each question is labeled as Understanding or Reasoning, enabling a detailed analysis of models' cognitive capabilities. We validate the MediQAl dataset through extensive evaluation with 14 large language models, including recent reasoning-augmented models, and observe a significant performance gap between factual recall and reasoning tasks. Our evaluation provides a comprehensive benchmark for assessing language models' performance on French medical question answering, addressing a crucial gap in multilingual resources for the medical domain.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper, "MediQAI: A French Medical Question Answering Dataset for Knowledge and Reasoning Evaluation":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MediQAI, a new French medical question answering dataset designed to assess the capabilities of language models in factual medical recall and reasoning within real-world clinical scenarios. The dataset contains 32,603 questions extracted from French medical licensing examinations. These questions cover 41 medical subjects. MediQAI includes three distinct tasks: multiple-choice questions with unique answers (MCQU), multiple-choice questions with multiple answers (MCQM), and open-ended questions with short answers (OEQ). Each question is labelled as requiring either "Understanding" or "Reasoning," allowing for a more detailed analysis of model cognitive abilities. The dataset is validated through extensive evaluation using 14 large language models, revealing a significant performance gap between factual recall and reasoning tasks. The authors make their dataset and evaluation scripts publicly available.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Novelty:** The paper addresses a significant gap by providing a French medical QA dataset.  Existing medical QA datasets are heavily English-centric, and French resources are scarce.  The inclusion of MCQU, MCQM, and OEQ tasks, alongside Understanding/Reasoning labels, provides a richer evaluation benchmark compared to datasets that solely focus on single-answer MCQs.
    *   **Significance:**  Medical AI research is rapidly expanding, and reliable evaluation resources are crucial.  MediQAI offers a new benchmark tailored to the French medical context, which is essential since medical practice, regulations, and educational systems vary across countries. The dataset's size (32,603 questions) and breadth of medical subjects (41) contribute to its usefulness. The paper thoroughly tests the dataset on a variety of established and recent LLMs.
    *   **Methodology:** The data collection and filtering processes seem rigorous.  The authors used multiple models to filter out easy questions for the test set, and used `gpt-40-2024-08-06` to assign medical subjects to the OEQ subset.
    *   **Reproducibility:** The authors are committed to reproducibility by releasing the dataset and the evaluation scripts.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited OEQ training data:** The OEQ task does not have its own dedicated training set. While the authors convert MCQU and MCQM examples to OEQ for training, this conversion may not fully capture the nuances of open-ended questions.
    *   **Automatic Understanding/Reasoning classification:** While the automatic classification of questions into "Understanding" and "Reasoning" is useful, the reported error rate of 8.4% is a concern. While this error is arguably low enough to provide useful insight, manual verification of labels may have strengthened the dataset.
    *   **Limited Clinical Focus in OEQ Filtering:** The paper mentions removing images and tables from the OEQ questions, and removing duplicates using cosine similarity. However, there is limited focus on ensuring the clinical validity of responses. For example, a LLM answer might be technically correct from a pure medical knowledge perspective, but not clinically appropriate in context.

*   **Significance and Influence:**

    *   MediQAI has the potential to stimulate research in French medical AI, facilitating the development and evaluation of language models specifically tailored for the French healthcare system. It provides a standardized benchmark for comparing different approaches and tracking progress.

    *   The dataset's structure, with its multi-task format and cognitive labels, can serve as a template for creating similar resources in other languages and domains.

    *   The performance analysis of various LLMs highlights the strengths and weaknesses of current models in the French medical context, guiding future research efforts.

**Overall Assessment:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of medical AI, particularly for French language models. The novelty lies in the creation and careful curation of a French medical QA dataset with diverse task formats and cognitive labels. The significance stems from the dataset's potential to accelerate research in French medical AI and provide a more representative benchmark for assessing language model capabilities in the French healthcare system. Despite minor weaknesses such as the automatic labeling process, the dataset is well-constructed and thoroughly evaluated. The availability of the data and scripts will promote reproducibility and facilitate further research.

**Score: 7.5**

The paper fills an important gap and demonstrates strong methodology, making it a significant contribution. However, certain aspects, such as the lack of an independent OEQ training set and focus on clinical validity of OEQ responses, prevent it from receiving a higher score. The potential for future impact within the French medical AI community, and its usefulness as a template for creating datasets in other languages/domains, provide the basis for a score slightly higher than average.
- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Pareto-Grid-Guided Large Language Models for Fast and High-Quality Heuristics Design in Multi-Objective Combinatorial Optimization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.20923v1)
- **Authors**: Minh Hieu Ha, Hung Phan, Tung Duy Doan, Tung Dao, Dao Tran, Huynh Thi Thanh Binh
- **Abstract**: Multi-objective combinatorial optimization problems (MOCOP) frequently arise in practical applications that require the simultaneous optimization of conflicting objectives. Although traditional evolutionary algorithms can be effective, they typically depend on domain knowledge and repeated parameter tuning, limiting flexibility when applied to unseen MOCOP instances. Recently, integration of Large Language Models (LLMs) into evolutionary computation has opened new avenues for automatic heuristic generation, using their advanced language understanding and code synthesis capabilities. Nevertheless, most existing approaches predominantly focus on single-objective tasks, often neglecting key considerations such as runtime efficiency and heuristic diversity in multi-objective settings. To bridge this gap, we introduce Multi-heuristics for MOCOP via Pareto-Grid-guided Evolution of LLMs (MPaGE), a novel enhancement of the Simple Evolutionary Multiobjective Optimization (SEMO) framework that leverages LLMs and Pareto Front Grid (PFG) technique. By partitioning the objective space into grids and retaining top-performing candidates to guide heuristic generation, MPaGE utilizes LLMs to prioritize heuristics with semantically distinct logical structures during variation, thus promoting diversity and mitigating redundancy within the population. Through extensive evaluations, MPaGE demonstrates superior performance over existing LLM-based frameworks, and achieves competitive results to traditional Multi-objective evolutionary algorithms (MOEAs), with significantly faster runtime. Our code is available at: https://github.com/langkhachhoha/MPaGE.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and a critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MPaGE, a novel framework that combines Large Language Models (LLMs) with a Pareto-Grid-guided evolutionary algorithm (SEMO) to design heuristics for multi-objective combinatorial optimization problems (MOCOPs). MPaGE aims to improve solution quality, runtime efficiency, and heuristic diversity.  It uses a Pareto Front Grid (PFG) to guide heuristic generation, clustering heuristics based on semantic similarity (using LLMs), and employing cross-cluster recombination guided by LLM feedback. The paper demonstrates MPaGE's superior performance compared to existing LLM-based and traditional multi-objective evolutionary algorithms (MOEAs) on several standard MOCOP benchmarks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**

    *   **Strengths:** The core novelty lies in its integrated approach: Combining LLMs with SEMO *and* a Pareto Front Grid for MOCOP is a relatively unique combination.  The emphasis on *semantic* diversity using LLMs to analyze code *logic* and *group heuristics*, and the use of *cross-cluster* recombination, are strong points of innovation.
    *   **Weaknesses:**  Individual components (using LLMs for heuristic generation, evolutionary strategies, Pareto grids) are not entirely novel in isolation. The framework builds upon existing techniques. The semantic diversity component also has similarities to AST-based methods; the key difference is the use of LLMs for the actual semantic analysis.

*   **Significance:**

    *   **Strengths:** The paper addresses a critical gap in the application of LLMs to optimization, moving beyond single-objective problems and addressing runtime efficiency and heuristic diversity, important aspects in real-world MOCOP applications.  The demonstrated improvements over existing LLM-based methods and competitive results with traditional MOEAs, while achieving faster runtime, indicate practical significance. This faster runtime is particularly important in situations where repeatedly evaluating the heuristic (as in SEMO) becomes a bottleneck.
    *   **Weaknesses:** The evaluation focuses on a limited set of standard MOCOP benchmarks.  It's unclear how well MPaGE would generalize to more complex, real-world problems with higher dimensionality or problem-specific constraints that might require very specialized heuristics. Also, while they mention that the prompts are available in the Appendix, including some actual LLM outputs (including prompts) and how they look like may make the analysis more clear.  Also, the paper could benefit from ablative studies to better understand the relative contributions of each component.
    * The reliance on GPT-4 mini and GPT-4 might pose a bottleneck for real-world problems since GPT-4 can be expensive. Also, this might create a reproducability issue that others who can't use GPT-4 can not reproduce the results.

*   **Rigorous Rationale:** The experiments are well-designed and the results are presented clearly, with appropriate metrics.  The comparison to a variety of baselines (both LLM-based and traditional MOEAs) strengthens the claims. However, more qualitative insights on the type of heuristics that MPaGE can produce (e.g., the heuristics code) may provide additional clarity on the performance.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a solid contribution to the field by effectively integrating LLMs into a multi-objective optimization framework. The emphasis on semantic diversity, guided search using Pareto grids, and the achieved runtime efficiency are notable strengths. However, the reliance on existing techniques, the limited scope of the benchmark problems, and the lack of real-world applicability insights prevent it from achieving a higher score. The potential for generalization to more complex problems and wider adaptation in real-world settings warrants consideration, yet limitations remain.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.NE
- **Score**: 7/10

### FHSTP@EXIST 2025 Benchmark: Sexism Detection with Transparent Speech Concept Bottleneck Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.20924v1)
- **Authors**: Roberto Labadie-Tamayo, Adrian Jaques Böck, Djordje Slijepčević, Xihui Chen, Andreas Babic, Matthias Zeppelzauer
- **Abstract**: Sexism has become widespread on social media and in online conversation. To help address this issue, the fifth Sexism Identification in Social Networks (EXIST) challenge is initiated at CLEF 2025. Among this year's international benchmarks, we concentrate on solving the first task aiming to identify and classify sexism in social media textual posts. In this paper, we describe our solutions and report results for three subtasks: Subtask 1.1 - Sexism Identification in Tweets, Subtask 1.2 - Source Intention in Tweets, and Subtask 1.3 - Sexism Categorization in Tweets. We implement three models to address each subtask which constitute three individual runs: Speech Concept Bottleneck Model (SCBM), Speech Concept Bottleneck Model with Transformer (SCBMT), and a fine-tuned XLM-RoBERTa transformer model. SCBM uses descriptive adjectives as human-interpretable bottleneck concepts. SCBM leverages large language models (LLMs) to encode input texts into a human-interpretable representation of adjectives, then used to train a lightweight classifier for downstream tasks. SCBMT extends SCBM by fusing adjective-based representation with contextual embeddings from transformers to balance interpretability and classification performance. Beyond competitive results, these two models offer fine-grained explanations at both instance (local) and class (global) levels. We also investigate how additional metadata, e.g., annotators' demographic profiles, can be leveraged. For Subtask 1.1, XLM-RoBERTa, fine-tuned on provided data augmented with prior datasets, ranks 6th for English and Spanish and 4th for English in the Soft-Soft evaluation. Our SCBMT achieves 7th for English and Spanish and 6th for Spanish.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper describes the FHSTP team's solutions for the EXIST 2025 sexism detection benchmark at CLEF 2025. The team tackled the task of identifying and categorizing sexism in social media textual posts, focusing on three subtasks: sexism identification, source intention, and sexism categorization. The core of their approach lies in using Speech Concept Bottleneck Models (SCBMs) and their extension, SCBMTs, along with a fine-tuned XLM-ROBERTa transformer model. SCBMs aim for interpretability by encoding input texts into human-understandable adjective-based representations. SCBMTs combine these representations with transformer embeddings. The team experimented with incorporating annotator demographics and undersampling strategies to improve performance. The results show that XLM-ROBERTa performed best in binary classification, while SCBMT was better suited for finer-grained categorization. The paper emphasizes the interpretability of the SCBM approach.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in the application of SCBMs, previously used for hate speech detection, to the domain of sexism detection. The incorporation of annotator demographics is also an interesting, though not entirely new, direction. Fusing the adjective representations with transformer embeddings in SCBMT is a useful extension. However, concept bottleneck models have been explored previously, so it's an incremental advancement. The use of GPT-3 mini for adjective generation is a standard approach, reducing its novelty.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the work lies in contributing to the ongoing effort to automatically detect and categorize sexism in social media. The interpretability of SCBM is a strong point, as it offers potential for understanding the reasoning behind the model's predictions, which is crucial for building trust and addressing biases. While the results of the model don't show state-of-the-art performance, its interpretability is still a key benefit.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and well-structured methodology.
    *   The focus on interpretability is valuable in the context of sexism detection.
    *   Thorough experimentation with different models, data augmentation techniques, and data balancing strategies.
    *   Exploration of incorporating annotator demographic information.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The performance of SCBM models, despite their interpretability, is not consistently superior to the baseline XLM-ROBERTa model. The results indicate it is only competitive on certain subtasks and datasets.
    *   The description of the LLM prompting could be more precise (i.e., hyperparameters and detailed prompt examples).
    *   The novelty is somewhat limited, as the core techniques have been used in other contexts.

*   **Potential Influence:** The work can influence future research by demonstrating the applicability of SCBMs in the sexism detection domain. The insights gained from analyzing the adjective-based explanations can inform the development of more robust and interpretable models. However, the limited performance improvement compared to simpler baselines might temper the enthusiasm for this specific approach. The work could also encourage more research on leveraging annotator demographics in social media analysis tasks.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a solid, well-executed study that makes a moderate contribution to the field of sexism detection. While the core methodology (SCBMs) isn't groundbreaking, its application to this specific problem and the emphasis on interpretability are valuable. The experimentation is thorough and the paper is clearly written. However, the limitations in performance and the incremental nature of the advancements prevent a higher score. A score of 6 reflects the paper's solid, yet not exceptional, contribution.

Score: 6
- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### FRED: Financial Retrieval-Enhanced Detection and Editing of Hallucinations in Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.20930v1)
- **Authors**: Likun Tan, Kuan-Wei Huang, Kevin Wu
- **Abstract**: Hallucinations in large language models pose a critical challenge for applications requiring factual reliability, particularly in high-stakes domains such as finance. This work presents an effective approach for detecting and editing factually incorrect content in model-generated responses based on the provided context. Given a user-defined domain-specific error taxonomy, we construct a synthetic dataset by inserting tagged errors into financial question-answering corpora and then fine-tune four language models, Phi-4, Phi-4-mini, Qwen3-4B, and Qwen3-14B, to detect and edit these factual inaccuracies. Our best-performing model, fine-tuned Phi-4, achieves an 8% improvement in binary F1 score and a 30% gain in overall detection performance compared to OpenAI-o3. Notably, our fine-tuned Phi-4-mini model, despite having only 4 billion parameters, maintains competitive performance with just a 2% drop in binary detection and a 0.1% decline in overall detection compared to OpenAI-o3. Our work provides a practical solution for detecting and editing factual inconsistencies in financial text generation while introducing a generalizable framework that can enhance the trustworthiness and alignment of large language models across diverse applications beyond finance. Our code and data are available at https://github.com/pegasi-ai/fine-grained-editting.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces FRED, a framework for detecting and editing factual hallucinations in language models, specifically focusing on financial text. It proposes a domain-specific error taxonomy and uses it to create a synthetic dataset by injecting tagged errors into existing financial QA datasets (FinQA and TAT-QA). The authors then fine-tune several small language models (SLMs) on this synthetic data, demonstrating improved performance in detecting and correcting factual inaccuracies compared to baseline LLMs like OpenAI's GPT-3.5 and GPT-4. The core contributions are a domain-specific error taxonomy, a methodology for synthetic dataset construction, and the evaluation of fine-tuned SLMs for financial hallucination detection and editing.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**

    *   The idea of detecting and editing hallucinations is not entirely novel. Many previous works have addressed this issue.
    *   However, the paper introduces a domain-specific error taxonomy tailored to the financial domain, which is a valuable contribution as it allows for more targeted and relevant hallucination detection.  Simply applying a general-purpose error taxonomy would likely be less effective.
    *   The synthetic dataset construction method is a practical approach, particularly given the scarcity of labeled real-world data. The careful insertion of tagged errors, along with filtering and correction steps, enhances the quality of the generated data.
    *   The evaluation of multiple SLMs is important, especially the comparison with larger LLMs.  The findings suggest that fine-tuned SLMs can achieve competitive or even superior performance within this specific domain.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The work has potential practical significance in the financial domain where accuracy and reliability are crucial. Hallucinations in financial reports or analysis could have severe consequences.
    *   The framework provides a practical recipe for improving the trustworthiness of language models in specialized domains by (1) defining domain-specific errors and (2) generating appropriate training data.
    *   The finding that fine-tuned SLMs can outperform or match the performance of larger models, at a potentially lower computational cost, is significant for practical deployment. This encourages adoption in scenarios where resource constraints are a concern.
    *   The paper presents the framework as generalizable beyond finance, although that claim is not strongly substantiated through experiments in other domains. Extending the framework to other high-stakes domains would increase its impact.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clearly defined problem and contributions.
    *   Well-structured methodology for dataset creation and model training.
    *   Comprehensive evaluation with multiple models and metrics.
    *   Focus on a practical, high-stakes domain (finance).

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Reliance on synthetic data. While practical, synthetic data may not fully capture the complexities and nuances of real-world financial text.
    *   Limited exploration of other domains. The claim of generalizability is not fully supported.
    *   The editing task is only evaluated using FActScoreLite. Using other automatic or human evaluation metrics would add additional validation.
    *   The gains over OpenAI's 03 are not consistent across all evaluations. While the Phi-4 model does perform better in detection, the 03 model is better at editing, which suggests there are important considerations about the trade-offs between each.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   The paper could encourage more research into domain-specific hallucination detection and editing techniques.
    *   The synthetic dataset construction method could serve as a template for other specialized domains.
    *   The success of fine-tuned SLMs could promote their adoption in resource-constrained environments.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **7**.

The paper offers a valuable contribution by focusing on a critical problem (hallucinations) within a high-stakes domain (finance). The domain-specific error taxonomy and synthetic dataset construction methodology are practical and well-executed. The results, showing that fine-tuned SLMs can compete with larger models, are significant. However, the reliance on synthetic data, the limited exploration of other domains, and the inconsistent gains over the LLMs slightly temper the overall impact. The paper is a solid piece of applied research with clear practical potential, but it could be strengthened by addressing the identified weaknesses. The editing metrics could be improved for additional validation.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Exploring text-to-image generation for historical document image retrieval
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.20934v1)
- **Authors**: Melissa Cote, Alexandra Branzan Albu
- **Abstract**: Attribute-based document image retrieval (ABDIR) was recently proposed as an alternative to query-by-example (QBE) searches, the dominant document image retrieval (DIR) paradigm. One drawback of QBE searches is that they require sample query documents on hand that may not be available. ABDIR aims to offer users a flexible way to retrieve document images based on memorable visual features of document contents, describing document images with combinations of visual attributes determined via convolutional neural network (CNN)-based binary classifiers. We present an exploratory study of the use of generative AI to bridge the gap between QBE and ABDIR, focusing on historical documents as a use case for their diversity and uniqueness in visual features. We hypothesize that text-to-image (T2I) generation can be leveraged to create query document images using text prompts based on ABDIR-like attributes. We propose T2I-QBE, which uses Leonardo.Ai as the T2I generator with prompts that include a rough description of the desired document type and a list of the desired ABDIR-style attributes. This creates query images that are then used within the traditional QBE paradigm, which compares CNN-extracted query features to those of the document images in the dataset to retrieve the most relevant documents. Experiments on the HisIR19 dataset of historical documents confirm our hypothesis and suggest that T2I-QBE is a viable option for historical document image retrieval. To the authors' knowledge, this is the first attempt at utilizing T2I generation for DIR.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the use of text-to-image (T2I) generation for historical document image retrieval (DIR). It proposes a new retrieval paradigm called T2I-QBE, where text prompts based on attributes (similar to those used in attribute-based document image retrieval - ABDIR) are used to generate query images. These generated images are then used as queries in a query-by-example (QBE) retrieval system. The authors tested different CNN architectures as feature extractors and various similarity measures, evaluating the performance on a historical document dataset (HisIR19).  The results show that the proposed approach is viable for historical DIR, although the performance is not as high as a fully supervised ABDIR approach.  The best performance was achieved using EfficientNet-B0 for feature extraction and L2 distance as the similarity measure.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using T2I generation to create query images for QBE DIR is moderately novel. While T2I is a rapidly evolving field, its application to DIR in this specific way (bridging ABDIR and QBE paradigms) appears to be a relatively new direction. The paper correctly identifies this as a first attempt. However, the overall methodology (using CNN features for image retrieval) is well-established.

*   **Significance:** The significance is arguably limited. The reported performance lags behind the ABDIR baseline, suggesting that the T2I-QBE method is not yet a practical replacement. The primary value lies in opening a potentially fruitful research avenue. The ability to generate queries based on abstract attributes offers a more flexible user interface than traditional QBE.
The biggest weakness is that the generated images are still synthetic and might not fully represent the characteristics of real document images, thus limiting the retrieval accuracy. The reliance on manually crafted prompts is another limitation; ideally, the system should be able to learn the relationship between document images and associated attributes. The experimental setup is not extensive. The choice of only one T2I generator (Leonardo.Ai) is restrictive.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-structured and easy-to-follow methodology.
    *   Quantitative evaluation with standard metrics.
    *   Identification of potential benefits of the approach (user flexibility).

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Limited novelty in terms of the underlying image retrieval methodology (CNN features).
    *   Subpar performance compared to the ABDIR baseline.
    *   Dependency on the quality and controllability of the T2I generator.
    *   Relatively small-scale experiments.
    *   The reliance on a single T2I model is restrictive. It's not clear how much the results depend on the specific characteristics of Leonardo.Ai.
    * Lack of comparison to traditional methods of creating queries, like manual image manipulation, to see if the T2I method is better than existing alternatives.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper might inspire further research into leveraging generative AI for DIR and related tasks. It highlights the potential of T2I for creating more flexible and intuitive retrieval systems.
*   **Rigor:** The experimental setup and methodology are sound. The paper has a standard experimental methodology and includes appropriate baseline architectures. The performance metrics are also suitable for the retrieval task.

**Overall:** The paper presents an interesting idea but lacks strong results to demonstrate its practical utility. The performance needs significant improvement before T2I-QBE becomes a competitive approach for historical DIR.

**Score: 5**

**Rationale:** The novelty is moderate as it combines existing techniques in a new way. The significance is limited by the performance gap to ABDIR. While the work has potential influence by opening a new direction, it lacks the transformative impact to justify a higher score. The methodology and experimentation are sound, but the lack of a significant performance gain limits the overall assessment.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 5/10

### Dissecting Persona-Driven Reasoning in Language Models via Activation Patching
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.20936v1)
- **Authors**: Ansh Poonia, Maeghal Jain
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) exhibit remarkable versatility in adopting diverse personas. In this study, we examine how assigning a persona influences a model's reasoning on an objective task. Using activation patching, we take a first step toward understanding how key components of the model encode persona-specific information. Our findings reveal that the early Multi-Layer Perceptron (MLP) layers attend not only to the syntactic structure of the input but also process its semantic content. These layers transform persona tokens into richer representations, which are then used by the middle Multi-Head Attention (MHA) layers to shape the model's output. Additionally, we identify specific attention heads that disproportionately attend to racial and color-based identities.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper investigates how large language models (LLMs) incorporate persona information into their reasoning process. The authors use activation patching on the LLaMA 3.2 1B Instruct model to understand how different model components, specifically MLP layers and attention heads, contribute to persona-driven variations in model outputs on the MMLU dataset. Their key findings include: 1) early MLP layers are not exclusively focused on syntax but also encode semantic features related to persona; 2) specific attention heads disproportionately attend to tokens associated with race-based persona cues; and 3) the semantic features are then passed through the layers influencing decision-making.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its targeted use of activation patching to dissect persona-driven reasoning *specifically*. While activation patching itself is not a new technique, the authors apply it to a specific and relevant problem: understanding how LLMs' internal circuitry encodes and utilizes persona information to influence their reasoning. This mechanistic approach is valuable. Also, finding that initial MLP layers are semantically relevant goes against earlier results.

*   **Significance:** The work is significant because understanding the mechanisms behind persona-driven behavior, especially when it leads to biases, is crucial for mitigating unwanted effects in LLMs. By identifying the specific layers and attention heads involved, the paper offers potential targets for intervention. The work also highlights the importance of investigating the role of seemingly low-level layers (early MLPs) in semantic processing.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Methodology:** The paper explains the activation patching methodology and experimental setup in a clear and replicable manner.
    *   **Focused Research Question:** The research question about persona-driven reasoning is well-defined and relevant.
    *   **Interesting Findings:** The findings regarding the early MLP layers and specific attention heads are intriguing and open up new avenues for research.
    *   **Use of Activation Patching:** The authors used the activation patching method very well to identify causally relevant parts of the network.
    *   **Detailed Investigation:** The study offers a thorough examination of the roles played by different components in LLMs and their connections.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope:** The study is limited to a single (relatively small) model (LLaMA 3.2 1B), and a specific dataset (MMLU). The findings may not generalize to other models or datasets, especially those where context or persona is less clearly defined.
    *   **Causality vs. Correlation:** While activation patching is designed to investigate causality, it can still be challenging to definitively prove causal relationships within the complex dynamics of an LLM. There's a risk that the observed correlations might be driven by other factors.
    *   **Simplistic Personas:** The defined personas are based on single words and phrases. While this allows for controlled experimentation, it may not fully capture the complexity of real-world personas.
    *   **Attention Head Analysis:** The attention head analysis seems a little vague due to the number of steps between identifying which heads to look at, then trying to evaluate their significance.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research on mechanistic interpretability and bias mitigation in LLMs. It motivates further investigation into the roles of early layers and specific attention heads in encoding and processing semantic information related to personas.

*   **Justification for Score:** The paper demonstrates novelty through its innovative integration of activation patching to dissect persona-driven reasoning within language models. By meticulously tracing the causal pathways of influence, it uncovers previously overlooked semantic processing within initial MLP layers and pinpoints attention heads that disproportionately focus on race-based cues. While the scope is confined to a specific model and dataset, the methodological rigor and intriguing insights provide valuable targets for future research in mechanistic interpretability and bias mitigation.

**Score: 7**

The paper offers valuable mechanistic insights into how LLMs handle persona information, demonstrating that early MLP layers are semantically relevant and identifying bias-prone attention heads. However, the limitations of the model scale, dataset, and persona complexity prevent the paper from reaching a higher score. Further research is needed to confirm the generality of these findings and to explore how they can be leveraged to improve the behavior of larger, more complex LLMs.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Mind the Gap: Conformative Decoding to Improve Output Diversity of Instruction-Tuned Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.20956v1)
- **Authors**: Max Peeperkorn, Tom Kouwenhoven, Dan Brown, Anna Jordanous
- **Abstract**: Instruction-tuning large language models (LLMs) reduces the diversity of their outputs, which has implications for many tasks, particularly for creative tasks. This paper investigates the ``diversity gap'' for a writing prompt narrative generation task. This gap emerges as measured by current diversity metrics for various open-weight and open-source LLMs. The results show significant decreases in diversity due to instruction-tuning. We explore the diversity loss at each fine-tuning stage for the OLMo and OLMo 2 models to further understand how output diversity is affected. The results indicate that DPO has the most substantial impact on diversity. Motivated by these findings, we present a new decoding strategy, conformative decoding, which guides an instruct model using its more diverse base model to reintroduce output diversity. We show that conformative decoding typically increases diversity and even maintains or improves quality.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the "diversity gap" in instruction-tuned large language models (LLMs), observing that instruction-tuning reduces the diversity of generated outputs compared to base models. This is explored using narrative generation tasks with various open-source and open-weight LLMs. The research identifies DPO (Direct Preference Optimization) as a key factor contributing to the diversity loss. To address this, the authors propose "conformative decoding," a novel decoding strategy that blends the probability distributions of the instruction-tuned model and its base model during generation, reintroducing diversity.  The experiments demonstrate that conformative decoding can improve diversity and even maintain (or slightly improve) output quality.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The concept of a "diversity gap" due to instruction tuning has been observed previously. However, this paper provides a more focused investigation into *why* this gap arises (specifically pointing to DPO) and offers a specific, model-agnostic method (conformative decoding) to mitigate it. The conformative decoding method itself, while relatively simple in its implementation, represents a novel and practical contribution. It attempts to balance the benefits of instruction tuning with the inherent diversity of the base model. The application of this method to narrative generation is also a valuable contribution, providing a solid experimental framework.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial issue: the trade-off between instruction following and diversity in LLMs.  If instruction-tuned models become too rigid and predictable, it limits their usefulness in creative tasks, exploration, and agent-based simulations. The findings have practical implications for developers and users of LLMs. If the conformative decoding technique gains traction it will certainly address some use cases.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The experimental setup is well-defined, and the use of pre-LLM era r/WritingPrompts is a very strong setup for the task, as it guarantees a human reference free from LLM influence.
    *   The paper explores the influence of different fine-tuning steps (SFT, DPO, RLVR) on output diversity, providing a more granular understanding of the diversity gap.
    *   The paper uses a variety of diversity metrics (Vendi Score, Truncated Entropy, Precision, Recall, MAUVE) to provide comprehensive measurement.
    *   The presentation of conformative decoding is clear and understandable, making it relatively easy to implement.
    *   The paper is well written, and provides solid grounding in literature with the references.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The improvement from conformative decoding, while positive, is fairly modest. More rigorous hyperparameter tuning of the mixing parameter (γ) or exploring more advanced truncation strategies might yield larger gains. The current hyperparameter selection is brief and could benefit from more detailed exploration.
    *   The study focuses primarily on smaller, open-source models. It is not clear how well conformative decoding would generalize to larger, closed-source models.  This is a major limitation given the increasing dominance of larger models in the field.
    *   The paper uses automatic metrics for evaluating diversity. While these are useful, human evaluation would further strengthen the claims, especially given the subjective nature of diversity and quality in narrative generation.
    *   The paper mentions that Llama-3.1-8B shows a negative trend to diversity, and then seems to suggest it might be a better model from the get go. More evidence will be required for this, and it requires greater discussion, or even omission for readability.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence the development of future decoding strategies for LLMs, encouraging a greater focus on preserving or reintroducing diversity without sacrificing instruction-following abilities. It could also prompt further research into the underlying causes of the diversity gap. The simplicity of the proposed technique makes it easy to adopt and experiment with, which should encourage its adoption in the LLM community. The paper could inspire more nuanced approaches to preference learning in LLMs.

*   **Score Justification:** While the paper addresses a well-known problem, it offers a concrete and relatively simple solution with compelling experimental evidence. The limitation to smaller models and the reliance on automatic evaluation metrics somewhat temper the overall impact. The core technique of conformative decoding is a valuable contribution, but more extensive validation is needed.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Your AI, Not Your View: The Bias of LLMs in Investment Analysis
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.20957v1)
- **Authors**: Hoyoung Lee, Junhyuk Seo, Suhwan Park, Junhyeong Lee, Wonbin Ahn, Chanyeol Choi, Alejandro Lopez-Lira, Yongjae Lee
- **Abstract**: In finance, Large Language Models (LLMs) face frequent knowledge conflicts due to discrepancies between pre-trained parametric knowledge and real-time market data. These conflicts become particularly problematic when LLMs are deployed in real-world investment services, where misalignment between a model's embedded preferences and those of the financial institution can lead to unreliable recommendations. Yet little research has examined what investment views LLMs actually hold. We propose an experimental framework to investigate such conflicts, offering the first quantitative analysis of confirmation bias in LLM-based investment analysis. Using hypothetical scenarios with balanced and imbalanced arguments, we extract models' latent preferences and measure their persistence. Focusing on sector, size, and momentum, our analysis reveals distinct, model-specific tendencies. In particular, we observe a consistent preference for large-cap stocks and contrarian strategies across most models. These preferences often harden into confirmation bias, with models clinging to initial judgments despite counter-evidence.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the research paper:

**Summary**

The paper investigates confirmation bias in Large Language Models (LLMs) applied to investment analysis.  It proposes a three-stage experimental framework to elicit and verify LLM biases towards key financial factors: sector, size (market capitalization), and momentum.  The framework involves generating balanced evidence for buy/sell recommendations, eliciting the LLM's preferences under contradictory information, and then verifying the resilience of those preferences against progressively stronger counter-evidence.  The key findings indicate that LLMs exhibit distinct, model-specific tendencies, particularly a preference for large-cap stocks and contrarian strategies.  Crucially, these preferences often harden into confirmation bias, making the models resistant to changing their initial judgments even with substantial counter-evidence. The paper also explored how the LLM's internal uncertainty relates to the strength of its preference for a particular stance.

**Critical Evaluation**

*Novelty:* The paper's primary novelty lies in its systematic investigation of confirmation bias within the context of LLM-based *investment analysis*. While prior work has explored biases in LLMs and their application to finance, this study offers a more realistic experimental setup using balanced and contradictory arguments, mirroring the complexities of real-world financial analysis. The explicit methodology to uncover and *quantify* latent biases in LLMs for financial applications is a strong contribution, differentiating it from studies that focus on broader LLM biases or simplified financial tasks.  The separation of the evidence generation LLM from the test LLMs also adds rigor.

*Significance:* The significance of this work stems from the increasing deployment of LLMs in autonomous financial systems.  The identification and quantification of confirmation bias represent a major risk to the trustworthiness and reliability of these systems.  The finding that LLMs can cling to initial judgments despite counter-evidence highlights a crucial area for improvement and mitigation. This study lays a vital foundation for developing strategies to reduce biases and increase transparency in LLM-driven investment services.  The entropy analysis revealing higher internal conflict in stronger biased models also offers a potentially valuable avenue for future research in detecting and mitigating biases. The paper's demonstration of a preference for large-cap stocks and contrarian strategies also presents specific practical implications for the financial industry.

*Strengths:*

*   **Rigorous Methodology:**  The three-stage framework provides a clear and systematic approach to eliciting and verifying biases.
*   **Realistic Testbed:**  Using balanced, contradictory arguments better simulates the challenges of real-world financial analysis compared to simpler single-sentence or single-indicator setups.
*   **Quantifiable Results:**  The paper provides quantifiable metrics (preference scores, flip rates, entropy) to measure and compare biases across different LLMs.
*   **Practical Implications:**  The findings have direct implications for the design and deployment of LLM-based financial services.
*   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-structured and clearly explains the methodology and results.

*Weaknesses:*

*   **Limited Scope:**  The study focuses on a specific set of financial factors (sector, size, momentum). The analysis could be extended to other relevant factors, such as value vs. growth investing, ESG considerations, geographic regions, management quality, etc.
*   **Simplified Evidence:**  The generated evidence, while controlled, is simplified and may not fully capture the nuance of real-world financial information. The intensity of evidence is represented by a single number, which is simplistic.
*   **Static Analysis:** The analysis is static and does not explore how biases might evolve over time or in response to market events.  There is no adaptation or mitigation attempted, only characterization.
*   **LLM Choice:** While varied, the set of evaluated LLMs could be expanded, particularly with newer, more advanced models. There is only one model evaluated per "family" which may not be sufficient.
*   **Lack of Mitigation:** The paper effectively demonstrates the problem, but it lacks any proposed solutions or mitigation strategies.

*Overall Assessment:*

The paper makes a valuable contribution to the understanding of biases in LLMs within the critical domain of investment analysis. The systematic methodology, realistic testbed, and quantifiable results provide compelling evidence of confirmation bias. While the scope is somewhat limited, the findings have significant practical implications and lay a strong foundation for future research.

Score: 7.5

- **Score**: 7/10

### PROVCREATOR: Synthesizing Complex Heterogenous Graphs with Node and Edge Attributes
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.20967v1)
- **Authors**: Tianhao Wang, Simon Klancher, Kunal Mukherjee, Josh Wiedemeier, Feng Chen, Murat Kantarcioglu, Kangkook Jee
- **Abstract**: The rise of graph-structured data has driven interest in graph learning and synthetic data generation. While successful in text and image domains, synthetic graph generation remains challenging -- especially for real-world graphs with complex, heterogeneous schemas. Existing research has focused mostly on homogeneous structures with simple attributes, limiting their usefulness and relevance for application domains requiring semantic fidelity. In this research, we introduce ProvCreator, a synthetic graph framework designed for complex heterogeneous graphs with high-dimensional node and edge attributes. ProvCreator formulates graph synthesis as a sequence generation task, enabling the use of transformer-based large language models. It features a versatile graph-to-sequence encoder-decoder that 1. losslessly encodes graph structure and attributes, 2. efficiently compresses large graphs for contextual modeling, and 3. supports end-to-end, learnable graph generation. To validate our research, we evaluate ProvCreator on two challenging domains: system provenance graphs in cybersecurity and knowledge graphs from IntelliGraph Benchmark Dataset. In both cases, ProvCreator captures intricate dependencies between structure and semantics, enabling the generation of realistic and privacy-aware synthetic datasets.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the PROVCREATOR paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces PROVCREATOR, a novel framework for synthesizing complex heterogeneous graphs with node and edge attributes.  The core idea is to formulate graph synthesis as a sequence generation task, enabling the use of transformer-based large language models (LLMs). PROVCREATOR features a graph-to-sequence encoder-decoder that losslessly encodes graph structure and attributes, efficiently compresses large graphs, and supports end-to-end learnable graph generation.  The framework is evaluated on system provenance graphs (cybersecurity) and knowledge graphs (IntelliGraph), demonstrating its ability to capture intricate dependencies between structure and semantics and generate realistic, privacy-aware synthetic datasets.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The central novelty lies in framing heterogeneous graph generation as a sequence generation problem suitable for leveraging LLMs. While other works have explored graph generation, this paper addresses the specific challenge of generating graphs with *complex*, *heterogeneous schemas*, including high-dimensional node and edge attributes.  The graph-to-sequence encoding scheme also seems to be a key contribution, designed for lossless representation and compact encoding, which are essential for LLM integration. Prior approaches focused on simple schemas or vector representations of node features, which do not capture dependencies as effectively.

*   **Significance:** Generating high-fidelity synthetic graphs for complex, real-world scenarios is a significant problem with broad applications. In cybersecurity, the scarcity of high-quality, shareable datasets limits the development of advanced ML-based defenses.  PROVCREATOR offers a path to overcome this limitation by creating synthetic provenance graphs that are realistic and privacy-preserving. Similar arguments hold for other domains relying on complex heterogeneous graph data. The ability to jointly model the graph structure *and* the attributes is also very important.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The clear problem definition and motivation (limitations of existing graph generation approaches).
    *   The innovative use of LLMs for complex graph generation.
    *   The sophisticated graph-to-sequence encoding scheme, designed for lossless representation and LLM compatibility.
    *   The empirical evaluation across two challenging domains (cybersecurity and knowledge graphs).
    *   The demonstrated ability to preserve class signal (in the downstream application task) when transferring from synthetic to real data.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the evaluation is thorough, the comparison against existing graph generation methods (GDSS) in some cases is limited to a third-party implementation due to scalability issues.  This might introduce some bias in the comparison. While the authors say they adjusted hyperparameters for the third-party implimentation it doesn't clarify what their metrics were for doing this.
    *   The paper could benefit from a more in-depth analysis of the limitations of PROVCREATOR, e.g., potential failure cases, sensitivity to hyperparameters, scalability, or ability to represent *dynamic* graphs.
    *   The validation of attributes relies on custom rules, which might not capture the full complexity of attribute dependencies. More sophisticated validation metrics could be considered.
    *   While LLMs are used for generation, they aren't leveraging techniques like in-context learning. The paper states the LLMs are fine-tuned on graph representations but no discussion of how this relates to prompting. The paper could further justify the architecture in relation to LLM best practices.
    *   The "zero" scores on some datasets are concerning and indicates limitations of the method as applied to that specific benchmark and could use more discussion.

*   **Potential Influence:**  PROVCREATOR has the potential to significantly impact the field of graph learning and synthetic data generation. The ability to create high-fidelity synthetic graphs could accelerate research in cybersecurity and other domains where data scarcity is a barrier.  The graph-to-sequence encoding scheme could also inspire new approaches to graph representation learning.

*   **Justification of Score:** The paper presents a novel and well-executed approach to a significant problem.  The empirical results demonstrate the effectiveness of PROVCREATOR in generating realistic synthetic graphs for complex domains. While there are some weaknesses (especially third-party comparison, the limited downstream application task, reliance on custom rules, and concerns about scalability), the overall contribution is substantial.
